), it suffices to solve a simpler “reduced” differential equation, stated in (
2.3.16
), and this simpler equation turns out to possess the
\displaystyle 0^{2+}i\omega
bifurcation from Remark
2.2
.
To convert (
2.1.1
) into (
2.3.16
), we make several changes of variables, and we summarize the related notation in Figure
5
.
We will apply Lombardi’s nanopteron theorem (Theorem
D.1
) to this simpler problem and then undo the reductions to recover solutions to the original problem.

(
2.1.1
)
\displaystyle U^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}_{0}U+\mu\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)U+\mathcal{Q}(U,\mu)\qquad\sigma(\mathcal{L}_{0})\cap i\mathbb{R}=0^{4+}i\omega

\displaystyle\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mu\chi_{0}+V

\displaystyle\displaystyle\mu

\displaystyle\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R},\ \chi_{0}^{*}[V]=0

(
2.3.6
)
\displaystyle V^{\prime}=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}V+\mu\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)V+\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}(V,\mu)\qquad\sigma(\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{0})\cap i\mathbb{R}=0^{3+}i\omega

\displaystyle\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\tau\chi_{3}+W

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tau

\displaystyle\displaystyle\in\mathbb{R},\ \chi_{0}^{*}[W]=\chi_{3}^{*}[W]=0

(
2.3.16
)
\displaystyle W^{\prime}=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}W+\mu\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)W+\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)\qquad\sigma(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0})\cap i\mathbb{R}=0^{2+}i\omega

Figure 5
.
The two reductions of the original system (
2.1.1
) to the simpler problem (
2.3.16
).
The center spectrum notation follows Remark
2.2
.

2.3.1.
Removal of translation invariance

By Hypothesis
4
, we can write any
\displaystyle U\in\mathcal{X}
uniquely as
\displaystyle U=\gamma\chi_{0}+V
, where
\displaystyle\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle\chi_{0}^{*}[V]=0
.
This motivates the decompositions

\widehat{\mathcal{X}}:=\!\left\{V\in\mathcal{X}\ \middle|\ \chi_{0}^{*}[V]=0\right\}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{D}}:=\mathcal{D}\cap\widehat{\mathcal{X}}.

The spaces
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{X}}
and
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
retain the norms of
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
, respectively.
Put

(2.3.1)

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}V:=\mathcal{L}_{0}V-\chi_{1}^{*}[V]\chi_{0}

and set

(2.3.2)

\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)V:=\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)V-\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)V]\chi_{0}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}(V,\mu):=\mathcal{Q}(V,\mu)-\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{Q}(V,\mu)]\chi_{0}.

Last, define

(2.3.3)

\Gamma_{\mu}(V):=\chi_{1}^{*}[V]+\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}(V),\qquad\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}(V):=\mu\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)V]+\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{Q}(V,\mu)],

and

(2.3.4)

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(V,\mu):=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}V+\mu\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)V+\widehat{\mathcal{Q}}(V,\mu).

Hypotheses
4
–
(i)
and
4
–
(ii)
then imply that (
2.1.2
) is equivalent to the decoupled system

(2.3.5)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\gamma^{\prime}(x)=\Gamma(V(x),\mu)

(2.3.6)

\displaystyle\displaystyle V^{\prime}(x)=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(V(x),\mu).

Given a solution
\displaystyle V
to (
2.3.6
), we can of course solve (
2.3.5
) for
\displaystyle\gamma
, up to a constant of integration
\displaystyle\gamma_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
:

(2.3.7)

\gamma(x)=\gamma_{0}+\int_{0}^{x}\Gamma_{\mu}(V(s))\ ds.

Then the full solution to (
2.1.2
) will have the form

U(x)=\gamma_{0}\chi_{0}+\underbrace{\left(\int_{0}^{x}\Gamma_{\mu}(V(s))\ ds\right)\chi_{0}+V(x)}_{{\displaystyle{U_{0}(x)}}}.

By Hypotheses
4
–
(i)
and
4
–
(ii)
, if a function
\displaystyle U
of this form solves (
2.1.2
), then so does
\displaystyle U_{0}
, which depends only on
\displaystyle V
.
Thus it suffices to solve only (
2.3.6
).
Manipulating the integral term that multiplies
\displaystyle\chi_{0}
in
\displaystyle U_{0}
will be one of our most delicate tasks later, in Sections
2.4.1
and
2.4.2
.

Our work here has followed
[
85
, Sec. 3.2]
; one difference is that we assume neither
\displaystyle\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)V]=0
nor
\displaystyle\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{Q}(V,\mu)]=0
, and so we must keep these terms in
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}
, and thus in
\displaystyle\Gamma
, unlike
[
85
, Eqn. (38)]
.
See Remark
3.9
for the precise reason, in the context of our dimer FPUT problems, why we do not assume that these functionals vanish on these kinds of inputs.
However, the terms in
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}
are small, and so their effect is negligible.
See also
[
39
, Sec. 3]
,
[
41
, Sec. II.C.1]
, and
[
35
, Sec. 2.2]
for the same sort of reduction of translation invariance.

Before proceeding, we note that
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
remains a first integral for (
2.3.6
), and we also prove some useful properties of
\displaystyle\Gamma
.

Lemma 2.5
.

With
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
defined in (
2.3.4
), we have
\displaystyle D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(V)\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(V,\mu)=0
for all
\displaystyle V\in\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
.

Proof.

Rewrite

\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(V,\mu)=\mathcal{F}(V,\mu)-\big(\chi_{1}^{*}[V]+\mu\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)V]+\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{Q}(V,\mu)]\big)\chi_{0}

and use Hypothesis
5
–
(ii)
and part
(iv)
of Lemma
2.3
.
∎

Lemma 2.6
.

The map
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}
, defined in (
2.3.3
), has the following properties.

(i)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}(\mathcal{S}{V})=\Gamma_{\mu}(V)
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}(\mathcal{S}{V})=\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}(\mathcal{S}{V})
for all
\displaystyle V\in\mathcal{X}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
\displaystyle f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{X}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible (in the sense of Definition
2.1
).
Then
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}\circ{f}
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}\circ{f}
are even.

(iii)

The map
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}
satisfies

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\mu<\mu_{0}\\
0<\|V\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq 1\\
0<\alpha,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}<1\end{subarray}}\big(\alpha\mu^{2}\big)^{-1}|\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}(\alpha\mu\tau_{1}\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\tau_{2}V)|<\infty

and

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\mu<\mu_{0}\\
0<\|V\|_{\mathcal{X}}+\|\grave{V}\|_{\mathcal{X}}<1\end{subarray}}(\mu^{2}\|\grave{V}\|_{\mathcal{X}})^{-1}|\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}(\mu{V}+\mu{\grave{V}})-\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}(\mu{V})|<\infty.

Proof.

(i)

This is a direct calculation using the definition of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}
, Hypotheses
2
and
4
–
(iii)
, and part
(ii)
of Lemma
2.3
.

(ii)

We have
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}(f(-x))=\Gamma_{\mu}(\mathcal{S}{f}(x))
since
\displaystyle f
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible, and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}(\mathcal{S}{f}(x))=\Gamma_{\mu}(f(x))
by part
(i)
.
The same holds for
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}
.

(iii)

This estimate follows from the definition of
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}
and the quadraticity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}
in its first variable.
∎

2.3.2.
Fixing a value for the first integral

We can write any
\displaystyle V\in\widehat{\mathcal{D}}
uniquely as
\displaystyle V=\tau\chi_{3}+W
, where
\displaystyle\tau\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle\chi_{3}^{*}[W]=0
, and so (
2.3.6
) is equivalent to

(2.3.8)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\tau^{\prime}(x)=\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\tau(x)\chi_{3}+W(x),\mu)\big]

(2.3.9)

\displaystyle\displaystyle W^{\prime}(x)=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\tau(x)\chi_{3}+W(x),\mu)-\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\tau(x)\chi_{3}+W(x),\mu)\big]\chi_{3}.

Now let

(2.3.10)

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}:=\!\left\{W\in\widehat{\mathcal{X}}\ \middle|\ \chi_{3}^{*}[W]=0\right\}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}:=\widehat{\mathcal{D}}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}.

We will show that the values of the first integral
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(V)
and the “orthogonal” component
\displaystyle W
completely determine
\displaystyle\chi_{3}^{*}(V)
, at least when everything is small.
In other words, we can solve
\displaystyle j=\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\tau\chi_{3}+W)
for
\displaystyle\tau
given small
\displaystyle\mu
,
\displaystyle j\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle W\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
.
Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
is constant on solutions of the coupled system (
2.3.8
)–(
2.3.9
) by Lemma
2.5
, this system therefore reduces to a problem in
\displaystyle W
alone, at least for “small-amplitude” solutions.
This is the same procedure that Iooss and James performed to obtain
[
41
, Eqn. (33)]
and likewise that Venney and Zimmer began at the start of
[
85
, Sec. 3.5]
.
However, these papers did not address a certain “consistency” problem with this approach as we do; see Lemma
2.8
and the comments preceding it.
Moreover, we need quite precise estimates on how
\displaystyle\tau
is so “completely determined” by the values of
\displaystyle W
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(V)
.
We mention that quite a related result appears in
[
35
, Lem. 4.1]
, albeit with the intention of different estimates and applications; our goals are sufficiently distinct as to warrant the full proof below.

Part
(iii)
of the following lemma makes precise how
\displaystyle W
and
\displaystyle J_{\mu}(V)
completely determine
\displaystyle\chi_{3}^{*}(V)
.
We also include several detailed expansions and estimates whose usefulness will only be apparent later.
We will work on the balls

\mathfrak{B}(r):=\!\left\{(W,j,\mu)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\mu_{0}]\ \middle|\ \max\{\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}},|j|,\mu\}<r\right\}.

Lemma 2.7
.

There exists
\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{T}}\in(0,\min\{\mu_{0},1\})
and an analytic map
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\colon\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{\mathcal{T}})\to\mathbb{R}
with the following properties.

(i)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)=0
for
\displaystyle 0\leq\mu\leq\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
.

(ii)

\displaystyle j=\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)
for
\displaystyle(W,j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{\mathcal{T}})
.

(iii)

\displaystyle\tau=\mathcal{T}(W,\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\tau\chi_{3}+W),\mu)
for
\displaystyle\max\{\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}},|\tau|,\mu\}<\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
.

(iv)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}{W},j,\mu)=\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)
for
\displaystyle(W,j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{\mathcal{T}})
.

(v)

\displaystyle D_{W}\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\grave{W}=-\frac{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\grave{W}}{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\chi_{3}}

and
\displaystyle D_{W}\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)\grave{W}=-\frac{\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\grave{W}}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}
for all
\displaystyle\grave{W}
,
\displaystyle\breve{W}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
and
\displaystyle(W,j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{\mathcal{T}})
.

(vi)

\displaystyle D_{WW}^{2}\mathcal{T}(0,0,0)[\grave{W},\breve{W}]=-D^{2}\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)[\grave{W},\breve{W}]+D^{2}\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)[\grave{W},\chi_{3}]D\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)\breve{W}
for all
\displaystyle\grave{W}
,
\displaystyle\breve{W}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
.

(vii)

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|\alpha|\leq 1\\
\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\mu_{\mathcal{T}}\\
0<\mu<\mu_{\mathcal{T}}\end{subarray}}\alpha^{-1}\mu^{-2}|\mathcal{T}(\alpha\mu{W},0,\mu)|<\infty
.

(viii)

\displaystyle\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<|\alpha|\leq 1\\
\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}}+\|\grave{W}\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\mu_{\mathcal{T}}\\
0<\mu<\mu_{\mathcal{T}}\end{subarray}}\mu^{-2}|\mathcal{T}(\alpha\mu{W}+\mu\grave{W},0,\mu)-\mathcal{T}(\alpha\mu{W},0,\mu)|<\infty
.

Proof.

We first construct the map
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
.
Put

(2.3.11)

\mathsf{J}(\tau,W,j,\mu):=j-\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\tau\chi_{3}+W),

so
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}
is real analytic on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\mu_{0}]
by Hypothesis
5
–
(i)
.
Hypothesis
5
–
(iv)
gives

(2.3.12)

\mathsf{J}(0,0,0,\mu)=-\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)=0

for all
\displaystyle\mu
, and part
(v)
of Lemma
2.3
implies

D_{\tau}\mathsf{J}(0,0,0,0)=-D\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)\chi_{3}=-1.

The analytic implicit function theorem then provides
\displaystyle\mu_{1}\in(0,\mu_{0}]
and
\displaystyle\mu_{2}>0
and an analytic map
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}\colon\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{1})\to(-\mu_{2},\mu_{2})
such that if
\displaystyle(W,j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{1})
and
\displaystyle|\tau|<\mu_{2}
, then
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}(\tau,W,j,\mu)=0
if and only if
\displaystyle\tau=\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)
.

Now we prove the more specialized properties of
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
.

(i)

The identity (
2.3.12
) implies that
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)=0
for all
\displaystyle\mu
.

(ii)

The identity
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu),W,j,\mu)=0
and the definition of
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}
in (
2.3.11
) give

j=\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W).

(iii)

Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)=0
by Hypothesis
5
–
(iv)
, the continuity result from Hypothesis
5
–
(i)
gives
\displaystyle\mu_{3}\in(0,\min\{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\})
such that if

(2.3.13)

\min\{\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}},|\tau|,\mu\}<\mu_{3}

then
\displaystyle|\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\tau\chi_{3}+W)|\leq\mu_{1}
.
Assume that (
2.3.13
) holds.
Then
\displaystyle(W,\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\tau\chi_{3}+W),\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{1})
and
\displaystyle|\tau|<\mu_{2}
, while a direct calculation yields
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}(\tau,W,\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\tau\chi_{3}+W),\mu)=0
.
Consequently, (
2.3.13
) forces

\tau=\mathcal{T}(W,\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\tau\chi_{3}+W),\mu).

(iv)

First, since
\displaystyle\|\mathcal{S}{W}\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}}
by Hypothesis
2
, we have
\displaystyle(\mathcal{S}{W},j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{1})
whenever
\displaystyle(W,j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{1})
.
Thus
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}{W},j,\mu)
is in fact defined for
\displaystyle(W,j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{1})
.
Next, we already know that
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}(W,\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu),j,\mu)=0
.
If we can also show that
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}(W,\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}{W},j,\mu),j,\mu)=0
, then we must have
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}{W},j,\mu)=\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)
.

The definition of
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}
in (
2.3.11
), Hypotheses
4
–
(iii)
and
5
–
(iii)
, and part
(ii)
of Lemma
2.3
enable us to calculate
\displaystyle\mathsf{J}(\mathcal{S}{W},\tau,j,\mu)=\mathsf{J}(W,\tau,j,\mu)
for any
\displaystyle\tau
.
Then

0=\mathsf{J}(\mathcal{S}{W},\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}{W},j,\mu),j,\mu)=\mathsf{J}(W,\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{S}{W},j,\mu),j,\mu),

as desired.

(v)

Part
(v)
of Lemma
2.3
tells us that
\displaystyle D\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)\chi_{3}=1
.
The analyticity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
then give
\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{T}}\in(0,\min\{\mu_{3},1\})
such that if
\displaystyle(W,j,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{\mathcal{T}})
, then
\displaystyle D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\chi_{3}\neq 0
.

Fix
\displaystyle j
,
\displaystyle\mu\in\mathbb{R}
with
\displaystyle\max\{|j|,\mu\}<\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
.
By part
(ii)
above, the map
\displaystyle\mathfrak{J}(W):=\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)
is constant for
\displaystyle\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}}<\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
, and so for any
\displaystyle\grave{W}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
, we have

(2.3.14)

0=D\mathfrak{J}(W)\grave{W}=D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\big[(D_{W}\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\grave{W})\chi_{3}+\grave{W}\big].

This implies

D_{W}\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\grave{W}=-\frac{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\grave{W}}{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\chi_{3}}.

In the special case
\displaystyle W=j=0
, we use the identity
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)
from part
(i)
above and the formula (
2.3.14
) to calculate

D_{W}\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)\grave{W}=-\frac{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(0)\grave{W}}{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(0)\chi_{3}}.

By Hypotheses
5
–
(iv)
and
5
–
(vi)
, we have

D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(0)U=D\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)U+\big(D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(0)-D\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)\big)U=\chi_{3}^{*}[U]+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)U

for any
\displaystyle U\in\mathcal{X}
.
Since
\displaystyle\chi_{3}^{*}[\grave{W}]=0
for
\displaystyle\grave{W}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
and
\displaystyle\chi_{3}^{*}[\chi_{3}]=1
, we conclude

D_{W}\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)\grave{W}=-\frac{\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)U}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}.

(vi)

This follows from part
(v)
and a lengthy calculation involving the product and chain rule in Banach spaces.

(vii)

Since
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)=0
, we may use the fundamental theorem of calculus twice and part
(v)
to write

\mathcal{T}(\alpha\mu{W},0,\mu)=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}D_{WW}^{2}\mathcal{T}(st\alpha\mu{W},0,\mu)[t\alpha\mu{W},\alpha\mu{W}]\ ds\ dt+D_{W}\mathcal{T}(0,0,\mu)(\alpha\mu{W})\\
=\alpha\mu^{2}\left(\alpha\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}D_{WW}^{2}\mathcal{T}(st\alpha\mu{W},0,\mu)[tW,W]\ ds\ dt-\frac{\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)W}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}\right).

The estimate then follows from the analyticity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
.

(viii)

Similar to part
(vii)
, we use the fundamental theorem of calculus twice to rewrite the difference
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(\alpha\mu{W}+\mu\grave{W},\mu,0)-\mathcal{T}(\alpha\mu{W},\mu,0)
in the form

\mu^{2}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\int_{0}^{1}D_{WW}^{2}\mathcal{T}(s(\alpha\mu{W}+t\mu\grave{W}),0,\mu)[\alpha{W}+t\grave{W},\grave{W}]\ ds\ dt-\frac{\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\grave{W}}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}\right).\qed

Now let
\displaystyle\mu_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mu_{2}
be as in Lemma
2.7
.
Suppose that
\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle W_{\mu}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
solve the system (
2.3.8
)–(
2.3.9
) with
\displaystyle 0\leq\mu\leq\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Put
\displaystyle j_{\mu}(x):=J_{\mu}(\tau_{\mu}(x)\chi_{3}+W_{\mu}(x))
, so
\displaystyle j_{\mu}
is constant by Hypothesis
5
–
(ii)
.
Suppose also that
\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}
and
\displaystyle W_{\mu}
are small enough that
\displaystyle\max\{\|W_{\mu}(x)\|_{\mathcal{X}},|\tau_{\mu}(x)|,\mu\}<\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
for all
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then part
(iii)
of Lemma
2.7
implies
\displaystyle\tau_{\mu}(x)=\mathcal{T}(W_{\mu}(x),j_{\mu}(0),\mu)
for all
\displaystyle x
.
That is, the coefficient on
\displaystyle\chi_{3}
is completely determined by the part of the solution orthogonal to
\displaystyle\chi_{3}
and the first integral — at least if everything is sufficiently small.

Conversely, this suggests a strategy for solving (
2.3.6
), equivalently the coupled system (
2.3.8
)–(
2.3.9
): assume
\displaystyle\tau(x)=\mathcal{T}(W(x),j,\mu)
for
\displaystyle j\in\mathbb{R}
fixed and attempt to solve

(2.3.15)

W^{\prime}(x)=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}(W(x),j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W(x),\mu)-\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}(W(x),j,\mu)\chi_{3}+W(x),\mu)\big]\chi_{3}.

This equation is now an ordinary differential equation in
\displaystyle W
with
\displaystyle\mu
and
\displaystyle j
as parameters.
Of course, here we assume that
\displaystyle W
,
\displaystyle j
, and
\displaystyle\mu
small enough for
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}(W(x),j,\mu)
to be defined.

This is precisely what we shall do, except there is a nagging question of consistency.
If
\displaystyle W
solves (
2.3.15
) and is sufficiently small, does putting
\displaystyle\tau(x):=\mathcal{T}(W(x),j,\mu)
solve (
2.3.8
)?
Happily, this is the case, at least if we pick
\displaystyle j=0
.
In other words, we are freezing the value of the first integral to be 0.

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose that
\displaystyle W\colon\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
solves (
2.3.15
) with
\displaystyle j=0
and
\displaystyle\max\{\|W(x)\|_{\mathcal{X}},\mu\}<\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
for all
\displaystyle x
.
Then the map
\displaystyle\tau(x):=\mathcal{T}(W(x),0,\mu)
solves (
2.3.8
).

Proof.

Since
\displaystyle(W(x),0,\mu)\in\mathfrak{B}(\mu_{\mathcal{T}})
for all
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}
, we may differentiate
\displaystyle\tau
directly using part
(v)
from Lemma
2.7
and find, suppressing all
\displaystyle x
-dependencies,

\tau_{\mu}^{\prime}=D_{W}\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)W^{\prime}=-\frac{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)W^{\prime}}{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\chi_{3}}.

We use (
2.3.15
) to calculate

D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)W^{\prime}\\
=D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W,\mu))\\
-\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W,\mu))\big]D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\chi_{3}.

Lemma
2.5
implies

D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W,\mu))=0,

and so

\tau_{\mu}^{\prime}=\frac{\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W,\mu))\big]D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\chi_{3}}{D\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W)\chi_{3}}\\
=\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu)\chi_{3}+W,\mu))\big]=\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\tau_{\mu}\chi_{3}+W,\mu)\big].\qed

2.3.3.
The fully reduced system

From now on, we assume
\displaystyle 0\leq\mu\leq\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
, where
\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
was defined in Lemma
2.7
, and, for
\displaystyle W\in\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
with
\displaystyle\|\mathcal{W}\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq\mu_{\mathcal{T}}
, we put

\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W):=\mathcal{T}(W,0,\mu),

with
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}
also defined in Lemma
2.7
.
We will study the “fully reduced” problem

(2.3.16)

W^{\prime}(x)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(W(x),\mu),

where

(2.3.17)

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(W,\mu):=\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mu)-\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\widehat{\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mu)\big]\chi_{3}.

The work in the previous two sections implies that if
\displaystyle W
solves (
2.3.16
) and is sufficiently small, then

(2.3.18)

U(x):=W(x)+\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W(x))\chi_{3}+\left(\int_{0}^{x}\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W(s))\chi_{3}+W(s)\big)\ ds\right)\chi_{0}

solves the original problem (
2.1.1
).

We rewrite
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
in the form

(2.3.19)

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(W,\mu)=\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}W+\mu\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)W+\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu),

where we use the definition of
\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{F}}
in (
2.3.4
) and its constituent terms from (
2.3.1
) and (
2.3.2
) to expand

(2.3.20)

\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}W:=\widehat{\mathcal{L}}_{0}W=\mathcal{L}_{0}W-\chi_{1}^{*}[W]\chi_{0},

(2.3.21)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)W

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)W-\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)W]\chi_{0}-\chi_{3}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)W]\chi_{3}-\left(\frac{\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)W}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}\right)\chi_{2}

\displaystyle-\mu\left(\frac{\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)W}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}\right)\big(\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)\chi_{3}-\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)\chi_{3}]\chi_{0}\big),

and

(2.3.22)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mu)-\chi_{0}^{*}\big[\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mu)\big]\chi_{0}

\displaystyle-\chi_{3}^{*}\big[\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mu)\big]\chi_{3}+\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(W,\mu)\chi_{2}

\displaystyle+\mu\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(W,\mu)\big(\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)\chi_{3}-\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)\chi_{3}]\chi_{0}\big),

with

\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(W,\mu):=\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)-D\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(0)W=\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)+\mu\left(\frac{\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)W}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}\right).

We have used the various hypotheses, part
(iii)
of Lemma
2.3
, and part
(v)
of Lemma
2.7
to simplify and cancel some terms to obtain the expansion (
2.3.19
).
Observe that

\chi_{k}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}W]=\chi_{k}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)W]=\chi_{k}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)]=0,\ k=0,3

for all
\displaystyle W\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
, and so
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
and its constituent terms do indeed map
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
into
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
.

The next lemma shows that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
satisfies all the properties of Theorem
D.1
, which guarantees nanopteron solutions to the reduced problem (
2.3.16
).

Lemma 2.9
.

For
\displaystyle 0\leq\mu\leq\mu_{1}
, the map
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
defined in (
2.3.19
) has the following properties.

(i)

The mappings
\displaystyle\mu\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)
and
\displaystyle(W,\mu)\mapsto\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)
are analytic with

\sup_{0\leq\mu\leq\mu_{1}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)\|_{\mathbf{B}(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})}\leq C\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\sup_{0\leq\mu\leq\mu_{1}}\|\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)\|_{\mathcal{X}}\leq C\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}.

(ii)

The symmetry
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
anticommutes with each term in
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
:

\mathcal{S}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}=-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\mathcal{S},\qquad\mathcal{S}\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)=-\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)\mathcal{S},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{S}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)=-\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(\mathcal{S}{W},\mu).

(iii)

The center spectrum of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
as an operator in
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
with domain
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
is

\sigma(\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0})\cap i\mathbb{R}=\{0,\pm{i}\omega\},

where
\displaystyle\pm{i}\omega
are the pure imaginary eigenvalues of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}
from Hypothesis
3
.
The point 0 is an eigenvalue of multiplicity 2 with generalized eigenvectors
\displaystyle\chi_{1}
and
\displaystyle\chi_{2}
satisfying
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\chi_{1}=0
,
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\chi_{2}=\chi_{1}
, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}\chi_{1}=\chi_{1}
.
The points
\displaystyle\pm{i}\omega
are eigenvalues of algebraic multiplicity 1.

(iv)

The spectral projection for
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
corresponding to 0 has the form

(2.3.23)

\widetilde{\Pi}_{0}W=\chi_{1}^{*}[W]\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}^{*}[W]\chi_{2},

where

(2.3.24)

\chi_{2}^{*}\big[\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(0)\chi_{1}\big]>0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\chi_{2}^{*}\big[D_{WW}^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(0,0)[\chi_{1},\chi_{1}]\big]\neq 0.

(v)

Let
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}
be the spectral projection for
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
corresponding to
\displaystyle\{0,\pm{i}\omega\}
and put

(2.3.25)

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{h}}:=(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}-\widetilde{\Pi})(\widetilde{\mathcal{X}})\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}:=\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{h}}\cap\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}.

Put
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\mathsf{h}}:=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}
, where
\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}
was defined in Hypothesis
7
, and take
\displaystyle b\in(0,\pi)
and
\displaystyle q\in(0,\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2})
from that hypothesis as well.
Then on the triple
\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\mathsf{h}},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{h}})
, the operator
\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{h}}}}
has the localized optimal regularity property with decay rate
\displaystyle q\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{-1/2}
and strip width
\displaystyle b
; the periodic optimal regularity property with base frequency
\displaystyle\omega
; and the suboptimal regularity property with growth rate
\displaystyle\grave{q}
.

Proof.

(i)

This is obvious from the definitions in (
2.3.20
), (
2.3.21
), and (
2.3.22
).

(ii)

This follows from Hypothesis
2
, Hypothesis
4
–
(iii)
, parts
(ii)
and
(vi)
of Lemma
2.3
, and part
(iv)
of Lemma
2.7
.

(iii)

This follows from Hypothesis
3
and Lemma
A.2
.

(iv)

That the spectral projection has this form follows from Lemma
A.2
.
For the inequality in (
2.3.24
), we use the definition of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
in (
2.3.21
) and the identities
\displaystyle\chi_{2}^{*}[\chi_{0}]=\chi_{2}^{*}[\chi_{3}]=0
to calculate

\chi_{2}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(0)\chi_{1}]=\chi_{2}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(0)\chi_{1}]-\mathscr{J}_{*}(0)\chi_{1}=\mathfrak{L}_{0}>0,

where the positivity is Hypothesis
6
.

The proof of the nonequality in (
2.3.24
) is slightly more involved.
From the definition of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
in (
2.3.19
), we calculate

\chi_{2}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(W,0)]=\chi_{2}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}W]+\chi_{2}^{*}\big[\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W)\big]+\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W).

We immediately have

\chi_{2}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}W]=\chi_{2}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{0}W]-\chi_{1}^{*}[W]\chi_{2}^{*}[\chi_{0}]=\chi_{3}^{*}[W]=0.

Next, we rewrite

\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,0)=\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W)+\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,0)

from the expansion of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}
in (
2.1.4
).

By parts
(i)
and
(v)
of Lemma
2.7
, we have

(2.3.26)

|\mathcal{T}_{0}(W)|\leq C\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}}^{2}

for
\displaystyle\|W\|_{\mathcal{X}}<\mu_{1}
.
The bilinearity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}
implies

(2.3.27)

\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W)=\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)^{2}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\chi_{3},\chi_{3})+2\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\chi_{3},W)+\mathcal{Q}_{0}(W,W).

The estimate (
2.3.26
) and the bilinearity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}
imply that the first two terms in (
2.3.27
) are quartic and cubic in
\displaystyle W
, respectively.
Next, the term
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,0)
is then cubic in
\displaystyle W
by (
2.1.5
) and the estimate (
2.3.26
).

We conclude

\displaystyle\displaystyle\chi_{2}^{*}\big[D_{WW}^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(0,0)[\chi_{1},\chi_{1}]\big]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=2\chi_{2}^{*}\big[\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\chi_{1},\chi_{1})\big]+D_{WW}^{2}\mathcal{T}(0)[\chi_{1},\chi_{1}]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=2\chi_{2}^{*}\big[\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\chi_{1},\chi_{1})\big]-D^{2}\mathcal{J}_{0}(0)[\chi_{1},\chi_{1}]

by part
(vi)
of Lemma
2.7
.
This final quantity is
\displaystyle 2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}\neq 0
, per Hypothesis
6
.

(v)

First, the spectral projection for
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
onto
\displaystyle\{0,\pm{i}\omega\}
has the form

\widetilde{\Pi}{W}=\widetilde{\Pi}_{0}W+\chi_{+}^{*}[W]\chi_{+}+\chi_{-}^{*}[W]\chi_{-},\qquad W\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}.

To verify this, use Lemma
A.2
and the uniqueness of the spectral projection discussed in Appendix
A.1
.
Then
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}{W}=\Pi{W}
for all
\displaystyle W\in\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}
, where
\displaystyle\Pi
was defined in (
2.1.10
).
It then follows from the definition of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}
in (
2.3.10
) that

\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{h}}=\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{h}},\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}=\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{h}},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\mathsf{h}}=\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{h}}.

The spaces
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{h}}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{h}}
were defined in (
2.1.11
) and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{h}}
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}
in (
2.3.25
).

We use (
2.3.21
) to calculate

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}-\widetilde{\Pi})\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)W

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{1}(\mu)W

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\left(\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)W-\mu\left(\frac{\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)W}{1+\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)\chi_{3}}\right)\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)\chi_{3}\right)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}

and (
2.3.22
) to calculate

\displaystyle\displaystyle(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}}-\widetilde{\Pi})\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}}(W,\mu)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\big(\mathcal{Q}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(W)\chi_{3}+W,\mu)+\mu\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{T}}(W,\mu)\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)\chi_{3}\big)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}},

with both inclusions from Hypothesis
7
.

Finally,
\displaystyle{\left.\kern-1.2pt\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}_{0}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathcal{L}_{0}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{h}}}}
, so we can just directly import the assumptions of Hypothesis
7
on the triple
\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}},\widetilde{\mathcal{Y}}_{\mathsf{h}},\widetilde{\mathcal{X}}_{\mathsf{h}})=(\mathcal{D}_{\mathsf{h}},\mathcal{X}_{\mathsf{h}},\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}})
.
∎

2.4.
The proof of Theorem
2.4

We will use the following term for brevity.

Definition 2.10
.

Let
\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}
be a Banach space.
A family of functions
\displaystyle f_{\mu}^{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{Y}
defined for
\displaystyle\mu\in\mathscr{I}_{1}\subseteq(0,1)
and
\displaystyle\alpha\in\mathscr{I}_{2}\subseteq\mathbb{R}
is
uniformly bounded
if

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu\in\mathscr{I}_{1}\\
\alpha\in\mathscr{I}_{2}\\
X\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}\|f_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}<\infty.

We say that the family is
uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized
if the estimate above is true when
\displaystyle\|f_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}
is replaced by
\displaystyle e^{q|X|}\|f_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)\|_{\mathcal{Y}}
, with
\displaystyle q\in\mathbb{R}
.

Now we begin the proof of Theorem
2.4
.
Lemma
2.9
allow us to invoke Theorem
D.1
to obtain nanopteron solutions to the fully reduced problem (
2.3.16
).
Specifically, these solutions have the form
\displaystyle W=\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
, where

(2.4.1)

\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x)=-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}\mu\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}x}{2}\right)\chi_{1}+\mu^{3/2}\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x)+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x))

and
\displaystyle\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is defined in (
2.2.3
).
Taking
\displaystyle W=\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
, where

(2.4.2)

\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x):=\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x),

also solves (
2.3.16
).

The maps
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
have the same properties as (their tilde-denuded counterparts) in Theorem
D.1
.
In particular,
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized, while
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{D}}_{\mathsf{h}}
is uniformly bounded.
Both maps are
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.
Also,

(2.4.3)

\chi_{0}^{*}[\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)]=\chi_{0}^{*}[\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)]=0.

All of the functions defined above are valid for
\displaystyle\mu
and
\displaystyle\alpha
in the ranges

(2.4.4)

0<\mu<\widetilde{\mu}_{*}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{\Alpha}_{0}\exp\left(-\frac{b\omega}{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}}\right)\leq\alpha\leq\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1},

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1}>0
is independent of
\displaystyle b
and
\displaystyle q
, while
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{*}
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Alpha}_{0}
may depend on
\displaystyle b
and
\displaystyle q
.
We may assume that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Alpha}_{0}
,
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1}
, and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mu}_{*}
are small enough that
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x))
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x))
are defined for all
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle\mu
and
\displaystyle\alpha
in the intervals (
2.4.4
).

It will also be convenient to abbreviate

(2.4.5)

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X):=-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}X}{2}\right)\chi_{1}+\mu^{1/2}\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X),

so that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized, while the solution
\displaystyle\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
has the “localized + (asymptotically) periodic structure”

(2.4.6)

\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x)=\mu\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x)+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x)).

In the following sections we will convert these solutions to the reduced problem (
2.3.16
) into solutions to the original problem (
2.1.1
) by undoing the changes of variables above.
The subsequent work is not particularly difficult, but we need to keep careful track of various powers of
\displaystyle\mu
and
\displaystyle\alpha
.

2.4.1.
Construction of the “periodic + growing” solutions
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mu}^{\alpha}

Per (
2.3.18
), since the map
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{P}}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s)=\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s)
solves the reduced problem (
2.3.16
), the map

(2.4.7)

\mathsf{P}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x):=\left(\int_{0}^{x}\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s)\big)\ ds\right)\chi_{0}+\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x))\chi_{3}\\
+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x)

solves the original problem (
2.1.1
).
We will rewrite this expression in the form (
2.2.8
).

We first put

(2.4.8)

\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(X):=\alpha^{-1}\mu^{-2}\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)).

Then
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}
is periodic, and it is uniformly bounded in the sense of Definition
2.10
by part
(vii)
of Lemma
2.7
.
It is also even, since the
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversibility of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
and part
(iv)
of Lemma
2.7
imply

(2.4.9)

\alpha\mu^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(-X)=\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(-X))=\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\mathcal{S}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))=\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)).

Next, we define

(2.4.10)

\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X):=\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(X)\chi_{3}+\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)

to see that
\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is uniformly bounded and

(2.4.11)

\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)=\alpha\mu\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X).

It remains for us to treat the integral in (
2.4.7
).
With
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}
defined in (
2.3.3
), we have

\Gamma_{\mu}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}\big(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)\big)=\alpha\mu\chi_{1}^{*}[\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)]+\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}\big(\mathcal{T}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X),\mu,0)\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)\big).

Set

(2.4.12)

\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\chi}(X):=\alpha^{-1}\chi_{1}^{*}[\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)].

This map is periodic and uniformly bounded by the expansions (
D.1.6
) and (
D.1.8
).
It is also even, by a calculation like (
2.4.9
).

Next, let

\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\Gamma}(X):=(\alpha\mu^{2})^{-1}\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)\big).

Part
(iii)
of Lemma
2.6
guarantees that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\Gamma}
is uniformly bounded, and it is also periodic.
Finally, it is even, since

\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}\big(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(-X))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(-X)\big)=\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\chi_{3}+\alpha\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)\big)

by the
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversibility of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
, part
(iv)
of Lemma
2.7
, part
(ii)
of Lemma
2.3
, and part
(i)
of Lemma
2.6
.

We now abbreviate

(2.4.13)

\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(X):=\big(\alpha\mu(\alpha+\mu)\big)^{-1}\big(\alpha^{2}\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\chi}(X)+\alpha\mu^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\Gamma}(X)\big),

so that
\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}
is, once again, periodic, uniformly bounded, and even.
We integrate and find

(2.4.14)

\int_{0}^{x}\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s)\big)\ ds=\alpha\mu^{1/2}(\alpha+\mu)\int_{0}^{\mu^{1/2}x}\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds.

The estimate (
2.2.6
) follows from all the uniform bounds mentioned above.
The Lipschitz estimate (
2.2.7
) follows from (
D.1.6
) and (
D.1.7
) and the various uniform bounds above.
Finally, the formula (
2.2.8
) follows from (
2.4.11
) and (
2.4.14
).

Remark 2.11
.

For the map
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
to be periodic, it is necessary and sufficient that the integral term in (
2.4.7
) be periodic.
Since the integrand is periodic and real analytic, the periodicity of the integral is equivalent to the vanishing of the zeroth Fourier coefficient of the integrand.
However, the integrand here is quite complicated due to the three terms in
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}
from (
2.3.3
), where now each of these terms is a composition involving both
\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{\mu}
and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
.
We know a great deal about these maps from Lemma
2.7
and part
(ii)
of Theorem
D.1
, but our present store of knowledge is not sufficient to help us divine whether or not the zeroth Fourier mode vanishes.

2.4.2.
Construction of the full “nanopteron + growing” solutions
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\mu}^{\alpha}

We may use (
2.3.18
) to see that, with
\displaystyle\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
defined in (
2.4.1
), the map

(2.4.15)

\mathsf{U}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x):=\left(\int_{0}^{x}\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s))\chi_{3}+\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s)\big)\ ds\right)\chi_{0}+\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x))\chi_{3}+\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x)

solves the original problem (
2.1.1
).
As in Section
2.4.1
, we rewrite a number of the terms above to highlight certain powers of
\displaystyle\mu
and
\displaystyle\alpha
, and now also with an eye toward separating exponentially localized terms from (asymptotically) periodic ones.

We begin this time with the integral in (
2.4.15
).
First, from the definitions of
\displaystyle\Gamma
and
\displaystyle\Gamma_{1}
in (
2.3.3
) and the definition of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\chi}
in (
2.4.12
), we calculate

(2.4.16)

\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s))\chi_{3}+\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s)\big)=-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}\mu\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}s}{2}\right)+\mu^{3/2}\chi_{1}^{*}[\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s)]\\
+\alpha^{2}\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\chi}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s))+\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s))\chi_{3}+\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s)\big).

Put

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(X):=\mu^{-2}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mu\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))-\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\big),

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
was defined in (
2.4.5
).
Part
(viii)
of Lemma
2.7
guarantees that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}
is uniformly bounded.
The methods of the preceding section also show that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}
is even.
Finally, the expansion of
\displaystyle\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
in (
2.4.6
) gives

\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(x))=\mu^{2}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(\mu^{1/2}x)+\alpha\mu^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}x)),

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}
was defined in (
2.4.8
).

Now set

\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\Gamma}(X):=\mu^{-2}\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}\big([\alpha\mu^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))]+[\mu^{2}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(X)\chi_{3}+\mu\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)]\big)\\
-\mu^{-2}\Gamma_{\mu}^{*}\big(\alpha\mu^{2}\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\chi_{3}+\alpha\mu\widetilde{\Phi}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X))\big).

Part
(iii)
of Lemma
2.6
implies that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\Gamma}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized in the sense of Definition
2.10
, and the methods of Section
2.4.1
show that it is even.
Last, abbreviate

\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(X):=\chi_{1}^{*}[\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)]+\mu^{1/2}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\Gamma}(X),

so
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized and even.

We may now use the work above and the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}
in (
2.4.13
) to rearrange (
2.4.16
) into

\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s))\chi_{3}+\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s)\big)=-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}\mu\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}s}{2}\right)+\mu^{3/2}\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(\mu^{1/2}s)\\
+\alpha\mu(\alpha+\mu)\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(\mu^{1/2}s)),

and so

(2.4.17)

\int_{0}^{x}\Gamma_{\mu}\big(\mathcal{T}_{\mu}(\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s))\chi_{3}+\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s)\big)\ ds=-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{0}}\mu^{1/2}\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}\mu^{1/2}x}{2}\right)+\mu\int_{0}^{\mu^{1/2}x}\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds\\
+\alpha\mu^{1/2}(\alpha+\mu)\int_{0}^{\mu^{1/2}x}\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s))\ ds.

We rewrite the remaining two integrals in (
2.4.17
) to isolate their leading-order behavior.
Part
(ii)
of Lemma
B.3
(with
\displaystyle\nu=\mu^{1/2}
and
\displaystyle q_{*}=\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}
) allows us to write

(2.4.18)

\int_{0}^{X}\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds=\Lup_{\mu,1}^{\alpha}\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}X}{2}\right)+\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,1}(X),

where
\displaystyle\Lup_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,1}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized.
Part
(i)
of Lemma
B.4
(now with
\displaystyle q_{*}=\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}/2
) allows us to write

(2.4.19)

\int_{0}^{X}\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(s))\ ds=\int_{0}^{\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X)}\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds+\mu^{1/2}\Lup_{\mu,2}^{\alpha,2}\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}X}{2}\right)+\mu^{1/2}\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,2}(X),

where
\displaystyle\Lup_{\mu}^{\alpha,\infty}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,2}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}
-localized, and therefore
\displaystyle q
-localized, since
\displaystyle 0<q<\mathfrak{L}_{0}^{1/2}
.

Put

\Lup_{\mu}^{\alpha}:=\Lup_{\mu}^{\alpha,1}+\alpha(\alpha+\mu)\Lup_{\mu}^{\alpha,2}

and

(2.4.20)

\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,0}(X):=\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,1}(X)+\alpha(\alpha+\mu)\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,2}(X)

Then
\displaystyle\Lup_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,0}
is uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized.
By inspection of (
2.4.18
) and (
2.4.19
), we see that
\displaystyle\partial_{X}[\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,0}]
is also uniformly bounded and
\displaystyle q
-localized.

Last, define

(2.4.21)

\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,*}(X):=\mu^{1/2}\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}(X)\chi_{3}+\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}(X).

By (
2.4.3
), we have
\displaystyle\chi_{0}^{*}[\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,*}(X)]=0
.
Then the definition of
\displaystyle\mathsf{W}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
in (
2.4.1
), the definition of
\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}
in (
2.4.10
), and the integral identity (
2.4.17
) allow us to rewrite the solution
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
from (
2.4.15
) into the version (
2.2.2
) foretold in the statement of Theorem
2.4
.

2.4.3.
Reversibility proofs

We show that each of the terms in the definition of
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
in (
2.2.2
) is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.
We will need the following lemma, whose proof is a direct calculation using Definition
2.1
and Hypothesis
4
–
(iii)
.

Lemma 2.12
.

Let
\displaystyle f\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a function and define
\displaystyle g_{k}(X):=f_{k}(X)\chi_{k}
, for
\displaystyle k=1,\ldots,4
, where
\displaystyle\chi_{k}
is one of the generalized eigenvectors from Hypothesis
4
.
Then
\displaystyle g_{k}
is reversible if
\displaystyle f
is an odd function and
\displaystyle k
is an even integer, or if
\displaystyle f
is an even function and
\displaystyle k
is an odd integer.

It follows from (
2.4.18
) and (
2.4.19
) and the evenness of
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}
and
\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}
that the maps
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu,1}^{\alpha,0}
and
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,1}
are odd.
Then from its definition in (
2.4.20
), the map
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,0}
is also odd.
Consequently, the prefactor functions on
\displaystyle\chi_{0}
in (
2.2.2
) are odd, and so Lemma
2.12
shows that the
\displaystyle\chi_{0}
-term in (
2.2.2
) is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.

Next, the
\displaystyle\sech^{2}
-prefactor on
\displaystyle\chi_{1}
is even, so that term in (
2.2.2
) is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.
Also, the map
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Sigma}_{\mu}^{\alpha,\mathcal{T}}
is even, so the
\displaystyle\chi_{3}
-term in the definition of
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,*}
in (
2.4.21
) is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.
Last, the map
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Upsilon}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible by Theorem
D.1
.
Thus
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,*}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.

Since
\displaystyle\Tup_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is odd, the last two terms in (
2.2.2
) will be
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible if we know that
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
, defined in (
2.2.8
), is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.
From its definition in (
2.4.10
), the map
\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible by the same arguments as for
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\mu}^{\alpha,*}
.
The
\displaystyle\chi_{0}
-term in (
2.2.8
) is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible by the evenness of the integrand
\displaystyle\Phi_{\mu}^{\alpha,\int}
and Lemma
2.12
.
Thus
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
is
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}
-reversible.

3.
The Position Traveling Wave Problem

We will reformulate the position traveling wave problem (
1.2.2
) for the general dimer into a first-order differential equation posed on an infinite-dimensional Banach space.
After choosing a small parameter that controls the distance of the wave speed from the speed of sound, we will show that this equation satisfies the hypotheses of Section
2.1
.
We are able to verify all the hypothesis except the symmetry for the general dimer; to obtain a symmetry, we must specialize to either a mass or a spring dimer.

We develop this first-order equation in Section
3.1
and introduce the near-sonic small parameter in Section
3.4
.
In between and afterward, we uncover a host of properties of this equation that are needed to verify the hypotheses.
Table
1
outlines precisely where the different hypotheses are considered for the concrete lattice problems.
Table
2
connects the notation of various operators and quantities in the abstract problem of Section
2
to the concrete lattice situation of this section.

), (
3.3.35
)

\displaystyle\chi_{k}^{*}[U]

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]

(
3.3.39
), (
3.3.40
), (
3.3.41
), (
3.3.42
)

\displaystyle\chi_{\pm}

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{\pm}(\kappa,w)

(
3.3.47
)

\displaystyle\chi_{\pm}^{*}[U]

\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{\pm}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]

Section
3.3.5

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(U)

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\kappa,w)

(
3.5.7
)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu;\kappa,w)

(
3.5.8
)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)

(
3.6.1
)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{0}

\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,w)

(
3.6.3
)

Table 2
.
Correspondence of notation between Sections
2
and
3

3.1.
The Iooss–Kirchgässner change of variables

Recall that
\displaystyle p_{1}
and
\displaystyle p_{2}
are the traveling wave profiles in position coordinates, and
\displaystyle p_{1}
and
\displaystyle p_{2}
must solve (
1.2.2
).
Following Iooss and Kirchgässner
[
43
, Sec. 2]
, we set
\displaystyle\xi_{j}=p_{j}^{\prime}
and then define functions
\displaystyle P_{j}
on
\displaystyle\mathbb{R}\times[-1,1]
by

(3.1.1)

P_{j}(x,v):=p_{j}(x+v).

Observe that

P_{j}(x,0)=p_{j}(x+0)=p_{j}(x)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\partial_{x}[P_{j}](x,v)=p_{j}(x+v)=\partial_{v}[P_{j}](x,v).

We collect these new coordinates into one vector as

(3.1.2)

\bm{U}(x):=\big(p_{1}(x),p_{2}(x),\xi_{1}(x),\xi_{2}(x),P_{1}(x,\cdot),P_{2}(x,\cdot)\big),

so that
\displaystyle\bm{U}(x)\in\mathcal{X}
, where

(3.1.3)

\mathcal{X}:=\!\left\{(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathcal{C}([-1,1])\times\mathcal{C}([-1,1])\ \middle|\ P_{1}(0)=p_{1},\ P_{2}(0)=p_{2}\right\}

We will be rather cavalier about whether we write elements of
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
as “row” or “column” vectors.
We will also work on the space

(3.1.4)

\mathcal{D}:=\mathcal{X}\cap\big(\mathbb{R}^{4}\times\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1])\times\mathcal{C}^{1}([-1,1])\big).

The spaces
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
are Banach spaces with the usual maximum norms.
For
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{X}
we will denote sometimes its components by
\displaystyle(\bm{U})_{j}
for
\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,6
, so that if
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
, then

(3.1.5)

(\bm{U})_{1}:=p_{1},\quad(\bm{U})_{2}:=p_{2},\quad(\bm{U})_{3}:=\xi_{1},\quad(\bm{U})_{4}:=\xi_{2},\quad(\bm{U})_{5}:=P_{1},\quad\text{and}\quad(\bm{U})_{6}:=P_{2}.

We are almost ready to express the traveling wave problem (
1.2.2
) as a first-order equation in
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
.
Define the evaluation operators
\displaystyle\delta^{\pm 1}
by

(3.1.6)

(\delta^{\pm 1}P_{j})(x,v):=P_{j}(x,\pm 1)=p_{j}(x\pm 1).

If
\displaystyle f=f(v)
is a function of a single variable
\displaystyle v
, we will still write
\displaystyle\delta^{\pm 1}f:=f(\pm 1)
.

The traveling wave problem (
1.2.2
) is then equivalent to

(3.1.7)

\bm{U}^{\prime}(x)=\mathcal{F}(\bm{U}(x);\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c),

where, for
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
, we define

(3.1.8)

\mathcal{F}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c):=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\xi_{2}\\
c^{-2}\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(\delta^{1}P_{2}-p_{1})-c^{-2}\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(p_{1}-\delta^{-1}P_{2})\\
c^{-2}w\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(\delta^{1}P_{1}-p_{2})-c^{-2}w\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(p_{2}-\delta^{-1}P_{1})\\
\partial_{v}[P_{1}]\\
\partial_{v}[P_{2}]\end{pmatrix}.

The parameter
\displaystyle w>0
is the (reciprocal of) the mass ratio, per (
1.1.8
), and the functions
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}
are the spring potentials from (
1.1.9
).

We can expand
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
as the superposition of linear, quadratic, and superquadratic terms:

(3.1.9)

\mathcal{F}(\bm{U};c)=\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}+c^{-2}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\bm{U};\beta,w)+c^{-2}\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{U};\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w).

We define these terms as follows.
First, the linear operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
is the block diagonal matrix

(3.1.10)

\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c):=\begin{bmatrix}0&\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}&0\\[10.0pt]
-c^{-2}(1+\kappa)\diag(1,w)&0&c^{-2}\Delta(\kappa,w)\\
0&0&\partial_{v}\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}:=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix},

and the component operator
\displaystyle\Delta(\kappa,w)
is

(3.1.11)

\Delta(\kappa,w):=\begin{bmatrix}0&\big(\delta^{1}+\kappa\delta^{-1}\big)\\
w\big(\kappa\delta^{1}+\delta^{-1}\big)&0\end{bmatrix}.

Next, the quadratic term
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}
is

(3.1.12)

\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\grave{\bm{U}};\kappa,\beta,w):=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
(\delta^{1}P_{2}-p_{1})(\delta^{1}\grave{P}_{2}-\grave{p}_{1})-\beta(p_{1}-\delta^{-1}P_{2})(\grave{p}_{1}-\delta^{-1}\grave{P}_{2})\\
w\beta(\delta^{1}P_{1}-p_{2})(\delta^{1}\grave{P}_{1}-\grave{p}_{2})-w(p_{2}-\delta^{-1}P_{1})(\grave{p}_{2}-\delta^{-1}\grave{P}_{1})\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Here we are writing
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}=(\grave{p}_{1},\grave{p}_{2},\grave{\xi}_{1},\grave{\xi}_{2},\grave{P}_{1},\grave{P}_{2})
.
Finally, the superquadratic term is

(3.1.13)

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{U};\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w)=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
\mathscr{V}_{1}(\delta^{1}P_{2}-p_{1})-\mathscr{V}_{2}(p_{1}-\delta^{-1}P_{2})\\
w\mathscr{V}_{2}(\delta^{1}P_{1}-p_{2})-w\mathscr{V}_{1}(p_{2}-\delta^{-1}P_{1})\\
\partial_{v}[P_{1}]\\
\partial_{v}[P_{2}]\end{pmatrix}.

The functions
\displaystyle\mathscr{V}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathscr{V}_{2}
represent the superquadratic terms in the spring forces, per (
1.1.9
).

The problem (
3.1.7
) is not the one to which we will apply our abstract theory from Section
2
.
In particular, we have not yet introduced a small parameter into (
3.1.7
); we will do so in Section
3.4
by taking the wave speed
\displaystyle c
to be sufficiently close to the speed of sound.
Before that, however, we develop some general properties of (
3.1.7
) that are valid for arbitrary
\displaystyle c
.

3.2.
Lattice symmetries

We first observe a symmetry at the level of the original position coordinates.
For a mass dimer (
\displaystyle m_{j+2}=m_{j}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{j}=\mathcal{V}_{0}
for all
\displaystyle j
), one can check that if
\displaystyle\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a solution set for (
1.1.4
), then so is
\displaystyle\{-u_{-j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
.
For a spring dimer (
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{j+2}=\mathcal{V}_{j}
and
\displaystyle m_{j}=m_{0}
for all
\displaystyle j
), the set
\displaystyle\{-u_{-j+1}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a solution whenever
\displaystyle\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is.
This mismatch in symmetries between the mass and spring dimers appears to be a perennial leitmotif of the respective traveling wave problems.
For example, at the level of relative displacement traveling waves, with the traveling wave profiles denoted by
\displaystyle\varrho_{1}
and
\displaystyle\varrho_{2}
as in (
1.2.3
), the articles
[
21
,
36
,
19
]
on the mass dimer assume that
\displaystyle\varrho_{1}
is even and
\displaystyle\varrho_{2}
is odd, while for the spring dimer
[
16
]
takes both
\displaystyle\varrho_{1}
and
\displaystyle\varrho_{2}
to be even.

We now define operators
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\in\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{X})
such that
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}^{2}=\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}^{2}=\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
anticommutes with the mass dimer’s system, and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
anticommutes with the spring dimer’s system.
That is, for the mass dimer, we need

(3.2.1)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}(1,w,c)\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{U}=-\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\mathcal{L}(1,w,c)\bm{U}\\
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{U},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{U};1,w)=-\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\bm{U};1,w)\\
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{U};\mathscr{V},\mathscr{V},w)=-S_{\mathbf{M}}\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{U},\mathscr{V},\mathscr{V},w).\end{cases}

For the spring dimer, we need

(3.2.2)

\begin{cases}\mathcal{L}(\kappa,1,c)\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\bm{U}=-\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\mathcal{L}(\kappa,1,c)\bm{U}\\
\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\bm{U},\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\bm{U};\beta,1)=-\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\bm{U};\beta,1)\\
\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\bm{U};\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},1)=-\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{U};\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},1).\end{cases}

To construct these symmetries, first define a “reflection” operator by

(3.2.3)

(Rf)(v):=f(-v),

where
\displaystyle f
is any function defined on
\displaystyle[-1,1]
.
The chain rule tells us that
\displaystyle R
and
\displaystyle\partial_{v}
anticommute.
With
\displaystyle(\delta^{\pm 1}f)(v):=f(\pm 1)
as in (
3.1.6
), we have
\displaystyle\delta^{\pm}R=\delta^{\mp}
.
Then the operators

(3.2.4)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}:=\begin{bmatrix}-\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}&0&0\\
0&\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}&0\\
0&0&-R\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}\end{bmatrix}

and

(3.2.5)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}:=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbb{J}&0&0\\
0&\mathbb{J}&0\\
0&0&-R\mathbb{J}\end{bmatrix},\qquad\mathbb{J}:=\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}

satisfy (
3.2.1
) and (
3.2.2
), respectively.
It is straightforward to check that the operator norms of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
are both 1.

The structures of these symmetries are related to our earlier observation that if
\displaystyle\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
solves (
1.1.4
), then
\displaystyle\{-u_{-j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is also a solution set for the mass dimer, while
\displaystyle\{-u_{-j-1}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a solution set for the spring dimer.
We view the reflection operator
\displaystyle R
in both cases as arising from the switching of
\displaystyle j
to
\displaystyle-j
, while the various factors of
\displaystyle-1
on the diagonal in both
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\displaystyle S_{\mathbf{K}}
come from the prefactor of
\displaystyle-1
on
\displaystyle u_{-j}
in the mass dimer and
\displaystyle u_{-j-1}
in the spring dimer.
Last, the “flip” operator
\displaystyle\mathbb{J}
for the spring dimer arises from the offset index
\displaystyle-j-1
.

We note with considerable interest, and frustration, that there does not appear to be a general symmetry that anticommutes with the terms of (
3.1.8
) when we are not specifically in the situation of a mass dimer or a spring dimer.
To be clear, we have
not
proved nonexistence of such a symmetry.
However, in light of the different even-odd and even-even symmetries for the prior relative displacement problems, and, indeed, the asymmetric appearance of the general dimer in Figure
1
, we think it is unlikely that such a symmetry exists.
This absence of symmetry is the chief reason why we report results separately in Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
for mass and spring dimers.
Nonetheless, we will be able to verify all of the non-symmetry hypotheses for the general dimer, which suggests that if a substitute could be found for the role of the symmetry in Lombardi’s methods, our methods would readily extend to the general dimer.

3.3.
Spectral analysis of the operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)

We exhaustively analyze the operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
, defined in (
3.1.10
) as an operator in the space
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
from (
3.1.3
) with domain
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
from (
3.1.4
).
In particular, we calculate its spectrum and a family of generalized eigenvectors associated with the eigenvalue 0.
We summarize all of our spectral-theoretic conventions in Appendix
A
.

3.3.1.
A characterization of the spectrum of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)

Let
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{C}
and suppose that
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{D}
and
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}=(\grave{p}_{1},\grave{p}_{2},\grave{\xi}_{1},\grave{\xi}_{2},\grave{P}_{1},\grave{P}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}

satisfy

z\bm{U}-\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}=\grave{\bm{U}}.

We will find a formula for
\displaystyle\bm{U}
in terms of
\displaystyle z
and
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}
; that is, we calculate the resolvent of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
.

Using the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
in (
3.1.10
), we first see that
\displaystyle P_{j}
,
\displaystyle\grave{P}_{j}
, and
\displaystyle p_{j}
must satisfy the initial value problems

\begin{cases}\partial_{v}[P_{j}]-z{P}_{j}=-\grave{P}_{j}\\
P_{j}(0)=p_{j}.\end{cases}

We solve them with Duhamel’s formula:

(3.3.1)

P_{j}(v)=e^{z{v}}p_{j}+\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{j}](v),

where, for
\displaystyle P\in\mathcal{C}([-1,1])
, we put

(3.3.2)

\mathcal{I}_{z}[P](v):=-\int_{0}^{v}e^{z(v-s)}P(s)\ ds,\qquad P\in\mathcal{C}([-1,1]).

Thus to find
\displaystyle P_{j}
it suffices to discover
\displaystyle p_{j}
.

Again using the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
in (
3.1.10
), we see that
\displaystyle p_{j}
,
\displaystyle\xi_{j}
, and
\displaystyle\grave{p}_{j}
must satisfy

z{p}_{j}-\xi_{j}=\grave{p}_{j},

and so

(3.3.3)

\xi_{j}=z{p}_{j}-\grave{p}_{j}.

Then
\displaystyle p_{j}
,
\displaystyle P_{j}
, and
\displaystyle\grave{\xi}_{j}
must satisfy

(3.3.4)

\begin{cases}z\xi_{1}+\left(\frac{1+\kappa}{c^{2}}\right)p_{1}-\left(\frac{\delta^{1}+\kappa\delta^{-1}}{c^{2}}\right)P_{2}=\grave{\xi}_{1}\\
\\
z\xi_{2}+\left(\frac{w(1+\kappa)}{c^{2}}\right)p_{2}-\left(\frac{w(\kappa\delta^{1}+\delta^{-1})}{c^{2}}\right)P_{1}=\grave{\xi}_{2}.\end{cases}

We calculate

\delta^{\pm 1}P_{j}=P_{j}(\pm 1)=e^{\pm{z}}p_{j}(x)+\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{j}](\pm 1)

and replace
\displaystyle P_{j}
and
\displaystyle\xi_{j}
in (
3.3.4
) with their
\displaystyle p_{j}
-dependent formulas to find

(3.3.5)

\begin{cases}z(z{p}_{1}-\grave{p}_{1})+\left(\frac{1+\kappa}{c^{2}}\right)p_{1}-\left(\frac{e^{z}+\kappa{e}^{-z}}{c^{2}}\right)p_{2}-\frac{\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{2}]+\kappa\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{2}]}{c^{2}}=\grave{\xi}_{1}\\
\\
z(z{p}_{2}-\grave{p}_{2})+\left(\frac{w(1+\kappa)}{c^{2}}\right)p_{2}-\left(\frac{\kappa{w}{e}^{z}+we^{-z}}{c^{2}}\right)p_{1}-\frac{\kappa{w}\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{1}]+w\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{1}]}{c^{2}}=\grave{\xi}_{2}.\end{cases}

After some rearrangements, this will be equivalent to a matrix-vector equation in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}^{2}
.
We define a matrix
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\in\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
by

(3.3.6)

\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c):=\begin{bmatrix}\big(c^{2}z^{2}+1+\kappa\big)&-\big(e^{z}+\kappa{e}^{-z}\big)\\[5.0pt]
-w\big(\kappa{e}^{z}+e^{-z}\big)&\big(c^{2}z^{2}+w(1+\kappa)\big)\end{bmatrix}

and an operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(z;\kappa,w,c)\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{C}^{2}
by

(3.3.7)

\mathcal{B}(z;\kappa,w,c)\grave{\bm{U}}:=\begin{pmatrix}c^{2}z\grave{p}_{1}+c^{2}\grave{\xi}_{1}+\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{2}]+\kappa\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{2}]\\[5.0pt]
c^{2}z\grave{p}_{2}+c^{2}\grave{\xi}_{2}+\kappa{w}\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{1}]+w\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[\grave{P}_{1}]\end{pmatrix}.

Then with
\displaystyle{\bf{p}}=(p_{1},p_{2})\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
, the system (
3.3.5
) becomes

(3.3.8)

\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c){\bf{p}}=\mathcal{B}(z;\kappa,w,c)\grave{\bm{U}}.

We can solve (
3.3.8
) uniquely for
\displaystyle{\bf{p}}
given
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}
if and only if

(3.3.9)

\det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\big)=c^{4}z^{4}+c^{2}(1+\kappa)(1+w)z^{2}+2\kappa{w}(1-\cosh(2z))\neq 0.

Since we can always solve for
\displaystyle P_{j}
and
\displaystyle\xi_{j}
via (
3.3.1
) and (
3.3.3
), we can solve
\displaystyle z\bm{U}-\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}=\grave{\bm{U}}
uniquely for
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{D}
given
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}\in\mathcal{X}
if and only if we can solve (
3.3.8
) for
\displaystyle{\bf{p}}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}
given
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}\in\mathcal{X}
.
And so we have the following characterization of the spectrum.

Proposition 3.1
.

The spectrum of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)\colon\mathcal{D}\to\mathcal{X}
is the set

(3.3.10)

\sigma(\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c))=\!\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\ \middle|\ \det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\big)=0\right\}.

Suppose
\displaystyle|c|>1
.
Then the following hold.

(i)

The spectrum consists entirely of eigenvalues.

(ii)

Each eigenvalue is an isolated point of the spectrum, and the algebraic multiplicity of an eigenvalue equals its multiplicity as a root of the map
\displaystyle\det\big(\mathcal{M}(\cdot;\kappa,w,c)\big)
.

(iii)

Each eigenvalue is geometrically simple.

(iv)

The spectrum is symmetric with respect to the real and complex axes in
\displaystyle\mathbb{C}
: if
\displaystyle z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c))
, then
\displaystyle\overline{z},-z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c))
, too.

(v)

Let
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
and
\displaystyle z\in\rho(\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c))
.
The six components of the resolvent operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}:=\big(z\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{D}}-\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)\big)^{-1}\bm{U}
are

(3.3.11)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{1}&=\frac{c^{2}z^{2}+w(1+\kappa)}{\det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\big)}\big(c^{2}\xi_{1}+c^{2}z{p}_{1}+\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{2}]+\kappa\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{2}]\big)\\
&+\frac{e^{z}+\kappa{e}^{-z}}{\det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\big)}\big(c^{2}\xi_{2}+c^{2}z{p}_{2}+\kappa{w}\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{1}]+w\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{1}]\big)\end{split}

(3.3.12)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\begin{split}\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{2}&=\frac{w(\kappa{e}^{z}+e^{-z})}{\det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\big)}\big(c^{2}\xi_{1}+c^{2}z{p}_{1}+\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{2}]+\kappa\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{2}]\big)\\
&+\frac{c^{2}z^{2}+1+\kappa}{\det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\big)}\big(c^{2}\xi_{2}+c^{2}z{p}_{2}+\kappa{w}\delta^{1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{1}]+w\delta^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{1}]\big)\end{split}

(3.3.13)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{3}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=z\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{1}-p_{1}

(3.3.14)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{4}

\displaystyle\displaystyle=z\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{2}-p_{2}

(3.3.15)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{5}(v)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e^{zv}\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{1}+\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{1}],\ -1\leq v\leq 1

(3.3.16)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{6}(v)

\displaystyle\displaystyle=e^{zv}\big(\mathcal{R}(z;\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}\big)_{2}+\mathcal{I}_{z}[P_{2}],\ -1\leq v\leq 1.

Proof.

For simplicity of notation in this proof, we suppress the dependence of (almost) all operators and functions on
\displaystyle c
,
\displaystyle\kappa
, and
\displaystyle w
; in particular, we just write
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(z;c)
for the matrix in (
3.3.6
) and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(c)
instead of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
.
The set equality (
3.3.10
) was established by the remarks preceding the statement of this proposition.

(i)

Let
\displaystyle z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
.
We first claim that if
\displaystyle|c|>1
and
\displaystyle z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
, then

(3.3.17)

c^{2}z^{2}+1+\kappa\neq 0.

Otherwise, if
\displaystyle c^{2}z^{2}+1+\kappa=0
, then we can solve for
\displaystyle z
as

z=\pm{i}\frac{\sqrt{1+\kappa}}{|c|}.

Since
\displaystyle z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
, we have

\det\left(\mathcal{M}\left(\frac{\sqrt{1+\kappa}}{|c|}\right);c\right)=0.

Some straightforward algebraic rearrangements then reveal that

(3.3.18)

\cos\left(\frac{2\sqrt{1+\kappa}}{|c|}\right)=-\left(\frac{\kappa^{2}+1}{2}\right)<-1,

which is impossible.

So, we may put

(3.3.19)

p_{1}=E(z;\kappa,c):=\frac{e^{z}+\kappa{e}^{-z}}{c^{2}z^{2}+1+\kappa}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad{\bf{p}}:=(E(z;\kappa,c),1).

Then
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(z;c){\bf{p}}=0
, and so with

(3.3.20)

\bm{E}=\bm{E}(z;\kappa,c):=(E(z;\kappa,c),1,zE(z;\kappa,c),z,E(z;\kappa,c)e^{z\cdot},e^{z\cdot}),

it is a direct calculation that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bm{E}=z\bm{E}
.
That is,
\displaystyle z
is an eigenvalue of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
.

(ii)

Suppose that
\displaystyle z\in\mathbb{C}\setminus\sigma(\mathcal{L})=\rho(\mathcal{L})
.
Using (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
), and (
3.3.8
) we can write the resolvent operator of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
at
\displaystyle z
in the form

(3.3.21)

\mathcal{R}(z)=\frac{1}{\det(\mathcal{M}(z;c))}\mathcal{R}_{1}(z)+\mathcal{R}_{2}(z),

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{1}
,
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}_{2}\colon\rho(\mathcal{L})\to\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{X})
are analytic.
From its definition in (
3.3.6
), we see that the mapping
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\cdot,z)\colon\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is entire, and so
\displaystyle\det(\mathcal{M}(\cdot;c))
is also entire.
Lemma
A.1
then guarantees that the eigenvalues of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}
are isolated and that the algebraic multiplicity of an eigenvalue equals its multiplicity as a root of
\displaystyle\det(\mathcal{M}(\cdot;c))
.

(iii)

Fix
\displaystyle z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
and suppose
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\bm{U}=z\bm{U}
for some
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
.
We use (
3.3.8
) with
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}=0
to obtain
\displaystyle p_{1}=E(z;\kappa,c)p_{2}
, with
\displaystyle E
defined in (
3.3.19
).
Then we use (
3.3.1
) and (
3.3.3
), again with
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}=0
, to solve for the other components of
\displaystyle\bm{U}
as

\xi_{1}=zE(z;\kappa,c)p_{2},\quad\xi_{2}=z{p}_{2},\quad P_{1}(v)=zE(z;\kappa,c)p_{2}e^{z{v}},\quad\text{and}\quad P_{2}(v)=p_{2}e^{z{v}}.

That is,

(3.3.22)

\bm{U}=p_{2}\big(E(z;\kappa,c),1,zE(z;\kappa,c),z,E(z;\kappa,c)e^{z\cdot},e^{z\cdot}\big)=p_{2}\bm{E}(z;\kappa,c)

as in (
3.3.20
), and so the eigenspace corresponding to
\displaystyle z
is 1-dimensional.

(iv)

The determinant (
3.3.9
) is even in
\displaystyle z
, so if
\displaystyle z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
, then
\displaystyle-z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
.
The coefficients in (
3.3.9
) are real and
\displaystyle\overline{\cosh(2z)}=\cosh(2\overline{z})
, so if
\displaystyle z\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
, then
\displaystyle\overline{z}\in\sigma(\mathcal{L})
.

(v)

This follows by unraveling the calculations in (
3.3.1
), (
3.3.3
), and (
3.3.8
).
∎

3.3.2.
The center spectrum of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)

We study
\displaystyle\sigma(\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c))\cap i\mathbb{R}
.
Given
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{R}
, we have
\displaystyle ik\in\sigma(\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c))
if and only if
\displaystyle k
satisfies the “dispersion relation”

(3.3.23)

0=\det(\mathcal{M}(ik;\kappa,w,c))=\underbrace{c^{4}k^{4}-c^{2}(1+w)(1+\kappa)k^{2}+2\kappa{w}(1-\cos(2k))}_{{\displaystyle{\Lambda(k;\kappa,w,c)}}}.

Clearly
\displaystyle\Lambda(0;\kappa,w,c)=0
; we are interested in the multiplicity of the root 0, and in the behavior of other roots of
\displaystyle\Lambda(\cdot;\kappa,w,c)
.

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\displaystyle\kappa
,
\displaystyle w>0
with
\displaystyle\max\{\kappa,w\}>1
.

(i)

There exists
\displaystyle c_{-}(\kappa,w)\in(0,1)
such that if
\displaystyle|c|\geq c_{-}(\kappa,w)
, then there exists a unique
\displaystyle\omega_{c}=\omega_{c}(\kappa,w)>0
such that
\displaystyle\Lambda(k;\kappa,w,c)=0
if and only if
\displaystyle k=0
or
\displaystyle k=\pm\omega_{c}(\kappa,w)
.

(ii)

The numbers
\displaystyle\pm\omega_{c}(\kappa,w)
from part
(i)
are both simple roots of
\displaystyle\Lambda(\cdot;\kappa,w,c)
.

(iii)

Set

(3.3.24)

c_{*}(\kappa,w):=\sqrt{\frac{4\kappa{w}}{(1+\kappa)(1+w)}}.

Then 0 has multiplicity 4 as a root of
\displaystyle\Lambda(\cdot;\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))
and multiplicity 2 as a root of
\displaystyle\Lambda(\cdot;\kappa,w,c)
when
\displaystyle c\neq c_{*}(\kappa,w)
.

(iv)

There exists
\displaystyle\lambda_{0}(\kappa,w)>0
such that if
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))=0
with
\displaystyle z\not\in i\mathbb{R}
, then
\displaystyle|\re(z)|\geq\lambda_{0}(\kappa,w)
.

Proof.

For simplicity throughout the proof, we will again suppress most notational dependencies of quantities on
\displaystyle c
,
\displaystyle\kappa
, and
\displaystyle w
.
In particular, we write
\displaystyle\Lambda(k;c)
instead of
\displaystyle\Lambda(k;\kappa,w,c)
and
\displaystyle c_{*}
for
\displaystyle c_{*}(\kappa,w)
.

(i)

The equation
\displaystyle\Lambda(k;c)=0
is really a quadratic equation in the unknown
\displaystyle c^{2}k^{2}
, and so by the quadratic formula we have
\displaystyle\Lambda(k;c)=0
if and only if

(3.3.25)

c^{2}k^{2}-\widetilde{\lambda}_{\pm}(k)=0,

where

(3.3.26)

\widetilde{\lambda}_{\pm}(k):=\frac{(1+\kappa)(1+w)}{2}\pm\frac{\sqrt{(1+w)^{2}(1-\kappa)^{2}+4\kappa((1-w)^{2}+4w\cos^{2}(k))}}{2}.

It was shown in
[
15
, Prop. 2.2.1, part (vii)]
that if at least one of
\displaystyle w
,
\displaystyle\kappa
is greater than 1, then there exists
\displaystyle c_{-}=c_{-}(\kappa,w)\in(0,1)
such that if
\displaystyle|c|>c_{-}
, then there is a unique positive solution
\displaystyle k=\omega_{c}=\omega_{c}(\kappa,w)
to (
3.3.25
).

(ii)

It was also shown in
[
15
, Prop. 2.2.1, part (vii)]
that

\inf_{|c|>c_{-}}|\partial_{k}[\Lambda](\omega_{c};c)|=\inf_{|c|>c_{-}}|2c^{2}\omega_{c}-\partial_{k}[\widetilde{\lambda}_{\pm}](\omega_{c})|>0.

Thus the roots
\displaystyle\pm\omega_{c}
are always simple.

(iii)

Since
\displaystyle\Lambda(\cdot;c)
is even, we have
\displaystyle\partial_{k}[\Lambda](0;c)=\partial_{k}^{3}[\Lambda](0;c)=0
.
And so we need to show
\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{2}[\Lambda](0;c_{*})=0
,
\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{4}[\Lambda](0;c_{*})\neq 0
, and
\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{2}[\Lambda](0;c_{*})\neq 0
for
\displaystyle c\neq c_{*}
.

We calculate

\partial_{k}^{2}[\Lambda](0;c)=8\kappa{w}-2c^{2}(1+w)(1+\kappa)

and

\partial_{k}^{4}[\Lambda](0;c)=24c^{4}-32\kappa{w}.

We see at once, then, that
\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{2}[\Lambda](0;c)=0
if and only if
\displaystyle|c|=c_{*}
.
In particular, 0 is a double root of
\displaystyle\Lambda(\cdot;c)
when
\displaystyle|c|>c_{*}
.

Next, taking
\displaystyle c=c_{*}
, we have

(3.3.27)

\partial_{k}^{4}[\Lambda](0;c_{*})=\frac{-32\kappa{w}\big((1+\kappa)^{2}w^{2}+(2\kappa^{2}-8\kappa+2)w+(1+\kappa)^{2}\big)}{(1+w)^{2}(1+\kappa)^{2}}

Consider the factor

(3.3.28)

(1+\kappa)^{2}w^{2}+(2\kappa^{2}-8\kappa+2)w+(1+\kappa)^{2}
