as a quadratic polynomial in
\displaystyle w
.
Its discriminant is

-48\kappa(\kappa^{2}-\kappa+1).

In turn, the discriminant of the quadratic factor
\displaystyle\kappa^{2}-\kappa+1
is
\displaystyle-3
.
It follows that

-48\kappa(\kappa^{2}-\kappa+1)<0

for all
\displaystyle\kappa>0
.
That is, the discriminant of (
3.3.28
) as a quadratic in
\displaystyle w
is negative.
Hence the quantity in (
3.3.28
) is nonzero for all
\displaystyle w\in\mathbb{R}
and, in fact, positive.
Returning to (
3.3.27
), we conclude
\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{4}[\Lambda](0;c_{*})<0
, and so 0 indeed has multiplicity 4 as a root of
\displaystyle\Lambda(\cdot;c_{*})
.

(iv)

The function
\displaystyle\mathcal{M}(\cdot;c_{*})
is (real) analytic, and so its zeros are isolated.
∎

For slightly greater notational simplicity, we will now write

(3.3.29)

\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w):=\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\omega_{*}(\kappa,w):=\omega_{c_{*}(\kappa,w)}(\kappa,w).

Remark 3.3
.

The value
\displaystyle\omega_{c}(1,w)
from part
(i)
of Proposition
3.2
is called
\displaystyle k_{c}
in the mass dimer paper
[
21
]
, where it appears in part (vi) of Lemma 2.1.
The value
\displaystyle\omega_{c}(\kappa,1)
is called
\displaystyle\Omega_{c}
in the spring dimer paper
[
16
]
, where it appears in part (v) of Proposition 2.1.

Part
(iii)
of Proposition
3.2
describes in part how the eigenvalue at 0 of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
perturbs when
\displaystyle|c|
is slightly greater than
\displaystyle c_{*}(\kappa,w)
: its multiplicity goes down by 2 from 4.
The next proposition completes that description: the multiplicity of the 0 eigenvalue goes down from 4 to 2 and two real eigenvalues of multiplicity 1 split off.
This behavior is sketched in Figure
6
(inspired by graphics like
[
85
, Fig. 1]
).

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\displaystyle\kappa
,
\displaystyle w>0
with
\displaystyle\max\{\kappa,w\}>1
.
There exist
\displaystyle\widetilde{c}_{*}(\kappa,w)>0
and
\displaystyle C_{\mathsf{x}}>0
such that if
\displaystyle c_{*}(\kappa,w)<|c|<\widetilde{c}_{*}(\kappa,w)
, then the following hold.

(i)

There exists a unique
\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{c}(\kappa,w)>0
such that for
\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}
,
\displaystyle\det(\mathcal{M}(x;\kappa,w,c))=0
if and only if
\displaystyle x=0
or
\displaystyle x=\pm\mathsf{x}_{c}(\kappa,w)
.

(ii)

The roots
\displaystyle\pm\mathsf{x}_{c}(\kappa,w)
are simple roots of
\displaystyle\det(\mathcal{M}(\cdot;\kappa,w,c))
.

(iii)

The roots
\displaystyle\pm\mathsf{x}_{c}(\kappa,w)
satisfy
\displaystyle|\mathsf{x}_{c}(\kappa,w)-\mathsf{x}_{c_{*}}|<C_{\mathsf{x}}|c^{-2}-c_{*}^{-2}|
.

The proof of this proposition is a careful, highly quantitative application of the intermediate value theorem, which we omit, since we do not use these eigenvalues
\displaystyle\pm\mathsf{x}_{c}(\kappa,w)
any further.
In principle we should also be able to calculate the leading order behavior in the small value
\displaystyle|c-c_{*}(\kappa,w)|
of
\displaystyle\mathsf{x}_{c}(\kappa,w)
using the sort of perturbation argument in
[
53
, Sec. 6.2]
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
\displaystyle c=c_{*}
\displaystyle i\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle+i\omega_{c_{*}}

\displaystyle-i\omega_{c_{*}}

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}
\displaystyle|c|\gtrsim c_{*}
\displaystyle i\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle+i\big(\omega_{c_{*}}+\mathcal{O}(\mu)\big)

\displaystyle-i\big(\omega_{c_{*}}+\mathcal{O}(\mu)\big)
\displaystyle\sqrt{\mu}\mathsf{x}_{c_{*}}+\mathcal{O}(\mu)
\displaystyle-\sqrt{\mu}\mathsf{x}_{c_{*}}+\mathcal{O}(\mu)

Multiplicity 4

Multiplicity 2

Multiplicity 1

Figure 6
.
Spectral behavior of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
for
\displaystyle c=c_{*}(\kappa,w)
and
\displaystyle|c|\gtrsim c_{*}(\kappa,w)
with
\displaystyle\mu=c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}-c^{-2}
.
For
\displaystyle|c|\gtrsim c_{*}(\kappa,w)
, the eigenvalue at 0 retains multiplicity 2 and two real eigenvalues of multiplicity 1 split off.

3.3.3.
The generalized eigenvectors of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
corresponding to 0

Since the eigenvalue at 0 of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
has algebraic multiplicity 4, we want four vectors
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}(\kappa,w)\in\mathcal{D}
such that

(3.3.30)

\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w)=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)\bm{\chi}_{k+1}(\kappa,w)=\bm{\chi}_{k}(\kappa,w),\ k=0,1,2.

Of course, such a Jordan chain always exists.
However, in the case of a mass or spring dimer, we will also want our eigenvectors to enjoy a special interaction with the symmetries (
3.2.4
) and (
3.2.5
), namely

(3.3.31)

\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{\chi}_{k}(1,w)=(-1)^{k+1}\bm{\chi}_{k}(1,w)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\bm{\chi}_{k}(\kappa,1)=(-1)^{k+1}\bm{\chi}_{k}(\kappa,1),

and not every Jordan chain for 0 must satisfy (
3.3.31
).

The components of these generalized eigenvectors are, unfortunately, rather overwhelming and depend in complicated ways on the parameters
\displaystyle\kappa
and
\displaystyle w
.
Recalling from (
3.1.3
) and (
3.1.4
) that the last two components of a vector in
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
are functions of the variable
\displaystyle v\in[-1,1]
, we specify what these functions are and then construct the other components out of them.
Broadly, these components resemble those of the generalized eigenvectors that our spatial dynamics predecessors have encountered, see, e.g.,
[
85
, Eq. (27)]
.
However, our generalized eigenvectors have quite a few more terms and depend wearingly on the parameters
\displaystyle\kappa
and
\displaystyle w
.
They are as follows:

(3.3.32)

\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w):=(1,1,0,0,1,1);

(3.3.33)

\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w):=\big(\chi_{15}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{16}(0;\kappa,w),1,1,\chi_{15}(\cdot;\kappa,w),\chi_{16}(\cdot;\kappa,w)\big),

where

\begin{cases}\chi_{15}(v;\kappa,w):=v+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right)\\
\\
\chi_{16}(v;\kappa,w):=v-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right);\end{cases}

(3.3.34)

\bm{\chi}_{2}(\kappa,w):=\big(\chi_{25}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{26}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{15}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{16}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{25}(\cdot;\kappa,w),\chi_{35}(\cdot;\kappa,w)\big),

where

\begin{cases}\chi_{25}(v;\kappa,w):=\frac{v^{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right)v+\frac{\kappa(1-w)}{(1+\kappa)^{2}(1+w)}\\
\\
\chi_{26}(v;\kappa,w):=\frac{v^{2}}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right)v-\frac{\kappa(1-w)}{(1+\kappa)^{2}(1+w)};\end{cases}

and

(3.3.35)

\bm{\chi}_{3}(\kappa,w):=\big(\chi_{35}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{36}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{25}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{26}(0;\kappa,w),\chi_{35}(\cdot;\kappa,w),\chi_{36}(\cdot;\kappa,w)\big),

where

\begin{cases}\chi_{35}(v;\kappa,w)=\frac{v^{3}}{6}+\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right)v^{2}+\frac{\kappa(1-w)}{(1+\kappa)^{2}(1+w)}v+\frac{(\kappa-1)(\kappa^{2}+14\kappa+1)}{24(1+\kappa)^{3}}\\
\\
\chi_{36}(v;\kappa,w)=\frac{v^{3}}{6}-\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right)v^{2}-\frac{\kappa(1-w)}{(1+\kappa)^{2}(1+w)}v-\left(\frac{(\kappa-1)(\kappa^{2}+14\kappa+1)}{24(1+\kappa)^{3}}\right).\end{cases}

Direct, and lengthy, calculations verify the identities (
3.3.30
) and (
3.3.31
).
Similar, and also omitted, calculations establish the following lemma, which shows that
\displaystyle\big(\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w),\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)\big)
is always a Jordan chain for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
, regardless of the value of
\displaystyle c
, while the nonlinearity
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}+\mathcal{Q}_{1}
is invariant under translation by
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\displaystyle\kappa
,
\displaystyle w>0
and
\displaystyle\beta
,
\displaystyle c\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

(i)

With
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
defined in (
3.1.10
), we have

\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w)=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)=\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w).

(ii)

With
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}
defined in (
3.1.12
) and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{1}
in (
3.1.13
), we have

\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U}+\gamma\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w),\bm{U}+\gamma\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w);\beta,w)=\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\bm{U};\beta,w)

and

\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{U}+\gamma\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w);\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w)=\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{U};\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w)

for all
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\displaystyle\gamma\in\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 3.6
.

We can view the high multiplicity of 0 as an eigenvalue and the structure of some of the generalized eigenvectors above as the inheritance of the system (
3.1.7
) of some properties of the original equations of motion (
1.1.4
).
Suppose that
\displaystyle\{u_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a solution set for the original equations of motion (
1.1.4
), not necessarily in the dimer or even polyatomic case.
Let
\displaystyle d_{1}
,
\displaystyle d_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\displaystyle d_{3}\in\mathbb{Z}
and define
\displaystyle\widetilde{u}_{j}(t):=u_{j+d_{3}}(t)+d_{1}t+d_{2}
.
Then
\displaystyle\{\widetilde{u}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
is also a solution set for (
1.1.4
).
In particular, the structure of
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}
and
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}
reflect the affine invariance of adding
\displaystyle d_{1}t+d_{2}
here.

3.3.4.
The projection onto the zero generalized eigenspace of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)

Since 0 is an isolated eigenvalue of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
, the spectral projection
\displaystyle\Pi_{0}(\kappa,w)
of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
corresponding to 0 is defined on
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
.
The image
\displaystyle\Pi_{0}(\kappa,w)(\mathcal{X})
is spanned by the four generalized eigenvectors
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}(\kappa,w)
from Section
3.3.3
, so we can express this projection in the form

(3.3.36)

\Pi_{0}(\kappa,w)\bm{U}=\sum_{k=0}^{3}\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]\bm{\chi}_{k}(\kappa,w),

where
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\cdot;\kappa,w]\colon\mathcal{X}\to\mathbb{R}
are linear functionals satisfying

\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\bm{\chi}_{j}(\kappa,w);\kappa,w]=\begin{cases}1,&j=k\\
0&j\neq k.\end{cases}

We can find explicit, although complicated, formulas for the coefficient functionals
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}
.

Proposition 3.7
.

Define

(3.3.37)

\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w):=\frac{4!}{\partial_{k}^{4}[\Lambda_{c_{*}(\kappa,w)}](0;\kappa,w)}=-\frac{3(1+\kappa)^{2}(1+w)^{2}}{\kappa{w}\big((1+\kappa)^{2}w^{2}+2(\kappa^{2}-4\kappa+1)w+(1+\kappa)^{2}\big)}

and

(3.3.38)

\mathsf{a}_{-2}(\kappa,w):=-\frac{4}{5}\left(\frac{\partial_{k}^{6}[\Lambda_{c_{*}(\kappa,w)}](0;\kappa,w)}{\big(\partial_{k}^{4}[\Lambda_{c_{*}(\kappa,w)}](0;\kappa,w)\big)^{2}}\right)=-\frac{(1+\kappa)^{4}(1+w)^{4}}{10\kappa{w}((1+\kappa)^{2}w^{2}+2(\kappa^{2}-4\kappa+1)w+(1+\kappa)^{2})}.

For simplicity, write
\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{-4}=\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)
,
\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{-2}=\mathsf{a}_{-2}(\kappa,w)
, and
\displaystyle c_{*}=c_{*}(\kappa,w)
.

Then the functionals
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}
that control the zero eigenprojection (
3.3.36
) satisfy the following formulas for
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
:

(3.3.39)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{c_{*}^{2}}{4}\big((2c_{*}^{2}+w(1+\kappa))\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}\big)p_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{c_{*}^{2}}{4}\big((2c_{*}^{2}+1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}\big)p_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{c_{*}^{2}w(\kappa-1)(\mathsf{a}_{-4}+6\mathsf{a}_{-2})}{12}\left(\xi_{1}-\frac{\xi_{2}}{w}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{\mathsf{a}_{-4}w}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)^{2}\left[\frac{(1+\kappa)(1-s)}{3}\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)+P_{2}(s)\big)+\frac{\kappa-1}{2}\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)-P_{2}(s)\big)\right]\ ds

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{\mathsf{a}_{-4}w}{2}\int_{-1}^{0}(1+s)^{2}\left[\frac{\kappa-1}{2}\big(P_{1}(s)-\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)+\frac{(1+\kappa)(1+s)}{3}\big(P_{1}(s)+\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)\right]\ ds

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{(\mathsf{a}_{-4}+6\mathsf{a}_{-2})(\kappa-1)w}{12}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)-P_{2}(s)\big)\ ds-\int_{-1}^{0}\big(P_{1}(s)-\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)\ ds\right]

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{w\big((2c_{*}^{2}+1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}\big)}{4}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\kappa(1-s)P_{1}(s)\ ds+\int_{-1}^{0}(1+s)P_{1}(s)\ ds\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{\big((2c_{*}^{2}+w(1+\kappa))\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}\big)}{4}\left(\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)P_{2}(s)\ ds+\int_{-1}^{0}\kappa(1+s)P_{2}(s)\ ds\right);

(3.3.40)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\frac{\mathsf{a}_{-4}c_{*}^{2}(\kappa-1)}{2}\left(p_{1}-\frac{p_{2}}{w}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{c_{*}^{2}\big((2c_{*}^{2}+w(1+\kappa))\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}\big)}{4}\xi_{1}

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{c_{*}^{2}\big((2c_{*}^{2}+1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}\big)}{4}\xi_{2}

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{\mathsf{a}_{-4}w}{2}\int_{0}^{1}(1-s)\bigg[(\kappa-1)\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)-P_{2}(s)\big)+(1+\kappa)(s-1)\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)+P_{2}(s)\big)\bigg]\ ds

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\frac{\mathsf{a}_{-4}w}{2}\int_{-1}^{0}(1+s)\bigg[(\kappa-1)\big(P_{1}(s)-\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)+(1+\kappa)(1+s)\big(P_{1}(s)+\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)\bigg]\ ds

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{w(2c_{*}^{2}+1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}}{4}\left(\int_{0}^{1}\kappa{P}_{1}(s)\ ds-\int_{-1}^{0}P_{1}(s)\ ds\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\frac{(2c_{*}^{2}+w(1+\kappa))\mathsf{a}_{-4}+4w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-2}}{4}\left(\int_{0}^{1}P_{2}(s)\ ds-\int_{-1}^{0}\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\ ds\right);

(3.3.41)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{2}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathsf{a}_{-4}c_{*}^{2}w\left[(1+\kappa)\left(p_{1}+\frac{p_{2}}{w}\right)+\frac{\kappa-1}{2}\left(\xi_{1}-\frac{\xi_{2}}{w}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\mathsf{a}_{-4}w\int_{0}^{1}\left[(1+\kappa)(s-1)\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)+P_{2}(s)\big)+\frac{\kappa-1}{2}\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)-P_{2}(s)\big)\right]\ ds

\displaystyle\displaystyle-\mathsf{a}_{-4}w\int_{-1}^{0}\left[(1+\kappa)(1+s)\big(P_{1}(s)+\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)+\frac{\kappa-1}{2}\big(P_{1}(s)-\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)\right]\ ds;

and

(3.3.42)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{3}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]

\displaystyle\displaystyle=\mathsf{a}_{-4}w(1+\kappa)c_{*}^{2}\left(\xi_{1}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{w}\right)

\displaystyle\displaystyle+\mathsf{a}_{-4}w(1+\kappa)\left(\int_{-1}^{0}\big(P_{1}(s)+\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)\ ds-\int_{0}^{1}\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)+P_{2}(s)\big)\ ds\right).

Proof.

As usual, we suppress most notational dependence on
\displaystyle\kappa
,
\displaystyle\beta
, and
\displaystyle w
in this proof.
Since 0 has algebraic multiplicity 4 as an eigenvalue of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}
, for
\displaystyle z\in\rho(\mathcal{L}_{0})
we have the Laurent series expansion at the pole
\displaystyle z=0

[
54
, eqn. (6.32)]
of the resolvent operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}
for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}
:

(3.3.43)

\mathcal{R}(z)=\frac{\mathcal{P}^{3}}{z^{4}}+\frac{\mathcal{P}^{2}}{z^{3}}+\frac{\mathcal{P}}{z^{2}}+\frac{\Pi_{0}}{z}+\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}z^{s}\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}^{s},

where
\displaystyle\Pi_{0}
is the spectral projection (
3.3.36
),
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{L}_{0}\Pi_{0}
, and
\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{R}}\in\mathbf{B}(\mathcal{X})
.
The operators
\displaystyle\Pi_{0}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}
commute.

Recall from (
3.1.5
) our convention of denoting the six components of
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{X}
by
\displaystyle(\bm{U})_{s}
for
\displaystyle j=1,\ldots,6
.
The generalized eigenvectors
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{j}
from (
3.3.32
), (
3.3.33
), (
3.3.34
), and (
3.3.35
) satisfy

(\bm{\chi}_{0})_{1}=(\bm{\chi}_{0})_{2}=1

and

(\bm{\chi}_{k})_{1}=-(\bm{\chi}_{k})_{2},\ k=1,2,3.

Consequently,

(\Pi_{0}\bm{U})_{1}+(\Pi_{0}\bm{U})_{2}=\sum_{k=0}^{3}\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\bm{U}](\bm{\chi}_{k})_{1}+\sum_{k=0}^{3}\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\bm{U}](\bm{\chi}_{k})_{2}=2\bm{\chi}_{0}^{*}[\bm{U}],

and thus

(3.3.44)

\bm{\chi}_{0}^{*}[\bm{U}]=\frac{(\Pi_{0}\bm{U})_{1}+(\Pi_{0}\bm{U})_{2}}{2}.

As in part
(i)
of Lemma
2.3
, we have the identities

(3.3.45)

\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{U}]=\bm{\chi}_{k+1}^{*}[\bm{U}],\ k=0,1,2,\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\bm{\chi}_{3}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{0}\bm{U}]=0.

Using the formula (
3.3.44
) after iterating (
3.3.45
) several times, along with the commutativity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{P}
and
\displaystyle\Pi_{0}
, yields

(3.3.46)

\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}[\bm{U}]=\bm{\chi}_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{U}]=\frac{(\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{U})_{1}+(\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{U})_{2}}{2},\ k=1,2,3.

For
\displaystyle k=1
,
\displaystyle 2
,
\displaystyle 3
, the number
\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{U})_{1}
is the coefficient of
\displaystyle z^{-(k+1)}
in the Laurent series (
3.3.43
) of
\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}(z)\bm{U})_{1}
, while
\displaystyle(\Pi_{0}\bm{U})_{1}
is the coefficient of
\displaystyle z^{-1}
in this series.
We can also calculate the Laurent series for
\displaystyle(\mathcal{R}(z)\bm{U})_{1}
directly from the formula in (
3.3.11
).
This formula is the sum of two
\displaystyle\bm{U}
-dependent terms, and each term is the quotient of an entire function of
\displaystyle z
divided by
\displaystyle\det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;c_{*})\big)
.
We then equate the explicit
\displaystyle\bm{U}
-dependent Laurent coefficients with
\displaystyle(\mathcal{P}^{k}\bm{U})_{1}
or
\displaystyle(\Pi_{0}\bm{U})_{1}
, as appropriate, and use the formula (
3.3.46
) to conclude the values of
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{k}^{*}
.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

By the proof of part
(iv)
of Proposition
3.2
, we have
\displaystyle\partial_{k}^{4}[\Lambda](0;\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))<0
, and so, from its definition in (
3.3.37
), we see that
\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)<0
.
We will need the negativity of
\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)
in Section
3.6.1
.

3.3.5.
The eigenvectors of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
corresponding to the eigenvalues
\displaystyle\pm{i}\omega_{*}(\kappa,w)

These eigenvalues are simple by part
(ii)
of Proposition
3.2
and
(iii)
of Proposition
3.1
.
By part
(i)
of Proposition
3.2
, these are the only eigenvalues of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
on the imaginary axis.
The one-dimensional eigenspaces corresponding to these eigenvalues are, by (
3.3.22
), spanned by the vectors

(3.3.47)

\bm{\chi}_{\pm}(\kappa,w):=\bm{E}(\pm{i}\omega_{*}(\kappa,w);\kappa,c_{*}(\kappa,w)),

where
\displaystyle\bm{E}
was defined in (
3.3.20
).

The spectral projections of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
corresponding to
\displaystyle\pm{i}\omega_{*}(\kappa,w)
then have the form
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{\pm}^{*}[\cdot;\kappa,w]\bm{\chi}_{\pm}(\kappa,w)
for certain functionals
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{\pm}^{*}[\cdot;\kappa,w]
.
We could calculate formulas for these functionals like those in Proposition
3.7
using the Laurent series of the resolvent of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
expanded at
\displaystyle\pm{i}\omega_{*}(\kappa,w)
and the formulas for the resolvent in part
(v)
of Proposition
3.1
.
However, we will not explicitly employ the functionals
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{\pm}^{*}[\cdot;\kappa,w]
in our subsequent work, and so we omit further calculations.

3.4.
The near-sonic small parameter

Now we are ready to introduce a small parameter
\displaystyle\mu
into the differential equation (
3.1.7
).
Because of the factor of
\displaystyle c^{-2}
that appears in the linear and quadratic terms of our traveling wave system in Section
3.1
, we take
\displaystyle\mu
to satisfy

\frac{1}{c^{2}}=\frac{1}{c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}}-\mu,\qquad 0\leq\mu\leq\frac{1}{2c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}}=\mu_{0}(\kappa,w).

Equivalently, the wave speed is now

(3.4.1)

\mathsf{c}_{\mu}(\kappa,w)^{2}:=\frac{c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}}{1-\mu c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}}=c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}+\mathcal{O}(\mu).

From (
3.1.10
), we can then express

(3.4.2)

\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,\mathsf{c}_{\mu}(\kappa,w))=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)+\mu\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w).

The operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
was defined in (
3.3.29
), while the new operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)
is

(3.4.3)

\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w):=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&0\\
(1+\kappa)\diag(1,w)&0&-\Delta(\kappa,w)\\
0&0&0\end{bmatrix},

The component operator
\displaystyle\Delta
was defined in (
3.1.11
).

For the nonlinear terms, first put

(3.4.4)

\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\grave{\bm{U}};\kappa,\beta,w):=c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\grave{\bm{U}};\beta,w),

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\grave{\bm{U}};\beta,w)
was defined in (
3.1.12
).
Then set

(3.4.5)

\mathcal{Q}(\bm{U},\mu;\kappa,\beta,\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w):=-\mu\mathcal{Q}_{0}(\bm{U},\bm{U};\beta,w)+\big(c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}-\mu\big)\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{U};\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w),

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{1}
was defined in (
3.1.13
).

With all of these definitions, our problem (
3.1.7
) is equivalent to

(3.4.6)

\bm{U}^{\prime}(x)=\mathcal{F}(\bm{U}(x),\mu;\kappa,\beta,\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w),

where

(3.4.7)

\mathcal{F}(\bm{U},\mu;\kappa,\beta,\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w):=\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)\bm{U}+\mu\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)\bm{U}+\mathcal{Q}(\bm{U},\mu;\kappa,\beta,\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w).

We will apply the abstract theory of Section
2
to the problem (
3.4.7
).
To do so, we must check that Hypotheses
1
through
7
hold.
By inspection, it is obvious that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)
are bounded operators from
\displaystyle\mathcal{D}
to
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
, where these Banach spaces were defined in (
3.1.3
) and (
3.1.4
).
The analyticity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}
in
\displaystyle\bm{U}
and
\displaystyle\mu
is also obvious, as are the bilinearity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}
and the cubicity of
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}-\mathcal{Q}_{0}
.
This verifies Hypothesis
1
for the equation (
3.4.6
).

Recall that in Section
3.2
we constructed symmetries
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
for the mass and spring dimer versions of (
3.1.9
), respectively.
The proofs that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(1,w)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,\mu;1,1,\mathscr{V},\mathscr{V},w)
anticommute with
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
and that
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,1)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}(\cdot,\mu;\kappa,\beta,\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},1)
anticommute with
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
are direct calculations, which we omit.
This verifies Hypothesis
2
in the separate mass and spring dimer cases.

We checked Hypothesis
3
on the center spectrum of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
in Section
3.3
, specifically in Proposition
3.2
.
Finally, in Sections
3.3.3
and
3.3.4
we proved Hypothesis
4
on the properties of the projection onto the zero eigenspace for
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
.

As for the remaining hypotheses, we will verify Hypothesis
5
on the first integral in Section
3.5
, Hypothesis
6
on the nondegeneracies in Section
3.6
, and Hypothesis
7
on optimal regularity in Section
3.7
.

Remark 3.9
.

The functional
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}^{*}[\cdot;1,w]
, defined in (
3.3.39
), vanishes on vectors
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{X}
of the form
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(0,0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},0,0)
.
The subspace of all vectors in
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
of this form, of course, contain the images of
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
under
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}
.
This is exactly what happens in the corresponding situations in
[
41
,
39
,
85
]
.
For the mass dimer, then, we might be tempted to add to Hypothesis
4
the conditions (using the language of Section
2
)

(3.4.8)

\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\mu)U]=\chi_{0}^{*}[\mathcal{Q}(U,\mu)]=0

for all
\displaystyle U
and
\displaystyle\mu
.
Doing so would simplify the function
\displaystyle\Gamma_{\mu}
in (
2.3.3
) considerably.
But the situation when
\displaystyle\kappa\neq 1
is not as nice, since, from their definitions in (
3.3.37
) and (
3.3.38
), it turns out that

\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)+6\mathsf{a}_{-2}(\kappa,w)>0.

Thus
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w]
is not automatically 0 for
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(0,0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},0,0)
and
\displaystyle\kappa
,
\displaystyle w
arbitrary, and so we do not add a condition like (
3.4.8
) to Hypothesis
4
.

3.5.
First integrals: verification of Hypothesis
5

The original equations of motion (
1.1.4
) form a Hamiltonian system; this is a key property of FPUT lattices in many treatments, see, e.g.,
[
70
, Ch. 1]
,
[
28
, Sec. 5.1]
, and
[
24
,
25
,
26
]
.
Unsurprisingly, then, the problem (
1.1.4
) possesses conserved quantities.

The precise first integral that we will need emerges at the level of the original position traveling wave problem (
1.2.2
).
To motivate how this first integral will behave in Iooss–Kirchgässner variables, we perform the following manipulations.
Divide the second equation in (
1.2.2
) by
\displaystyle w
and add this rescaled equation to the first to find that the position traveling wave profiles
\displaystyle p_{1}
and
\displaystyle p_{2}
must satisfy

(3.5.1)

c^{2}\left(p_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{p_{2}^{\prime\prime}}{w}\right)-(1-S^{-1})\big[\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(S^{1}p_{2}-p_{1})+\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(S^{1}p_{1}-p_{2})\big].

For
\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, define

(3.5.2)

(\mathcal{I}_{-}f)(x):=\int_{-1}^{0}f(x+s)\ ds.

After differentiating under the integral and invoking the fundamental theorem of calculus, we have

\partial_{x}\mathcal{I}_{-}=1-S^{-1}.

Then (
3.5.1
) reads

\partial_{x}\left[c^{2}\left(p_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{p_{2}^{\prime}}{w}\right)-\mathcal{I}_{-}\big[\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(S^{1}p_{2}-p_{1})+\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(S^{1}p_{1}-p_{2})\big]\right]=0,

and so the map

x\mapsto c^{2}\left(p_{1}^{\prime}(x)+\frac{p_{2}^{\prime}(x)}{w}\right)-\int_{-1}^{0}\big[\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(p_{2}(x+s+1)-p_{1}(x+s))+\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(p_{1}(x+s+1)-p_{2}(x+s))\big]\ ds

is constant when
\displaystyle p_{1}
and
\displaystyle p_{2}
solve (
1.2.2
).

We can translate this into a first integral for the equation (
3.1.7
) using the Iooss–Kirchgässner variables from Section
3.1
.
The key observation is that
\displaystyle p_{j}(x+s+1)=P_{j}(x,s+1)
, per (
3.1.1
).
We define this first integral precisely and present some of its fundamental properties.

Proposition 3.10
.

Given
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
, set

(3.5.3)

\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c):=c^{2}\left(\xi_{1}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{w}\right)-\int_{-1}^{0}\big[\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(P_{2}(s+1)-P_{1}(s))+\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(P_{1}(s+1)-P_{2}(s))\big]\ ds.

(i)

The map
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}\colon(\bm{U},c)\mapsto\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)
is analytic and, with
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)
defined in (
3.1.8
),

D\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)\mathcal{F}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)=0

for all
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{X}
.

(ii)

\displaystyle D\mathcal{J}(0;\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)\bm{U}=c^{2}\xi_{1}+\frac{c^{2}}{w}\xi_{2}+\int_{-1}^{0}\big(P_{1}(s)+\kappa{P}_{2}(s)\big)\ ds-\int_{0}^{1}\big(\kappa{P}_{1}(s)+P_{2}(s)\big)\ ds

(iii)

In the special cases of the mass and spring dimers,
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}
is invariant under the symmetries from Section
3.2
:

\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{U};\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V},w,c)=\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V},w,c)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},1,c)=\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},1,c),

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
was defined in (
3.2.4
) and
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{K}}
in (
3.2.5
).

(iv)

With
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w)
defined in (
3.3.32
), we have

\mathcal{J}(\bm{U}+\mu\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,w);\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)=\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)

for all
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{X}
and
\displaystyle\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.

(v)

Let

(3.5.4)

\mathscr{J}_{w}\bm{U}:=\xi_{1}+\frac{\xi_{2}}{w}.

Then
\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{w}
is a bounded linear functional on
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
and

(3.5.5)

\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)-\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,\grave{c})=(c^{2}-\grave{c}^{2})\mathscr{J}_{w}\bm{U}.

Proof.

(i)

For
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})
,
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}=(\grave{p}_{1},\grave{p}_{2},\grave{\xi}_{1},\grave{\xi}_{2},\grave{P}_{1},\grave{P}_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
, it is straightforward to calculate

(3.5.6)

D\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)\grave{\bm{U}}=c^{2}\left(\grave{\xi}_{1}+\frac{\grave{\xi}_{2}}{w}\right)-\int_{-1}^{0}\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(P_{2}(s+1)-P_{1}(s))(\grave{P}_{2}(s+1)-\grave{P}_{1}(s))\ ds\\
-\int_{-1}^{0}\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(P_{1}(s+1)-P_{2}(s))(\grave{P}_{1}(s+1)-\grave{P}_{2}(s))\ ds.

Now we take
\displaystyle\grave{\bm{U}}=\mathcal{F}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)
.
Per the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{F}
in (
3.1.8
), set

\grave{\xi}_{1}=c^{-2}\mathcal{V}_{1}(P_{2}(1)-p_{1})-c^{-2}\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(p_{1}-P_{2}(-1)),

\grave{\xi}_{2}=c^{-2}w\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime}(P_{1}(1)-p_{2})-c^{-2}w\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(p_{2}-P_{1}(-1)),

and
\displaystyle\grave{P}_{j}=P_{j}^{\prime}
.
Then the first integral in (
3.5.6
) becomes

\int_{-1}^{0}\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(P_{2}(s+1)-P_{1}(s))(\grave{P}_{2}(s+1)-\grave{P}_{1}(s))\ ds=\int_{-1}^{0}\partial_{s}[\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(S^{1}P_{2}-P_{1})]\ ds\\
=\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(P_{2}(1)-P_{1}(0))-\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(P_{2}(0)-P_{1}(-1))=\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(P_{2}(1)-p_{1})-\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime}(p_{2}-P_{1}(-1)),

where we have used the definition of the space
\displaystyle\mathcal{X}
to find
\displaystyle P_{j}(0)=p_{j}
.
Consequently,

c^{2}\grave{\xi}_{1}-\int_{-1}^{0}\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(P_{2}(s+1)-P_{1}(s))(\grave{P}_{2}(s+1)-\grave{P}_{1}(s))\ ds=0.

A similar cancelation occurs when we add
\displaystyle c^{2}\grave{\xi}_{2}/w
to the second integral in (
3.5.6
).

(ii)

This is a direct calculation using (
3.5.6
) and the identities
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{1}^{\prime\prime}(0)=1
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\kappa
, per (
1.1.9
).
Substitute
\displaystyle u=s+1
in the second integral to obtain the desired formula.

(iii)

For the mass dimer, using the definition of
\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}
in (
3.2.4
), we have

\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{U};\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V},w,c)=c^{2}\left(\xi+\frac{\xi_{2}}{w}\right)+\int_{-1}^{0}\big[\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(-P_{2}(-s-1)+P_{1}(-s))+\mathcal{V}^{\prime}(-P_{1}(-s-1)+P_{2}(-s))\big]\ ds.

Substitute
\displaystyle u=-s-1
in the integral to conclude
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(\mathcal{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{U};\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V},w,c)=\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V},\mathcal{V},w,c)
.
The proof for the spring dimer uses exactly the same substitution.

(iv)

This is a direct calculation.

(v)

This is a direct calculation.
∎

This first integral
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}
is not quite the right one to use in conjunction with Hypothesis
5
, since we want our first integral to interact in a special way with the functional
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{3}^{*}[\cdot;\kappa,w]
.
Instead, we simultaneously rescale
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}
by
\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)
, as defined in (
3.3.37
), and select the wave speed to be
\displaystyle c=\mathsf{c}_{\mu}(\kappa,w)
.
Define

(3.5.7)

\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\kappa,w):=w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)\mathcal{J}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,\mathsf{c}_{\mu}(\kappa,w)).

It is then straightforward to calculate that the rescaled first integral
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
satisfies all the conditions of Hypothesis
5
for the near-sonic problem (
3.4.6
).
In particular, we may use the expression for
\displaystyle D\mathcal{J}(0;\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)
given in part
(ii)
above and the formula for
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{3}^{*}[\cdot;\kappa,w]
given in (
3.3.42
) to find

D\mathcal{J}_{0}(0;\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\kappa,w)\bm{U}=w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)D\mathcal{J}(0;\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))\bm{U}=\bm{\chi}_{3}^{*}[\bm{U};\kappa,w].

Also, put

(3.5.8)

\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu;\kappa,w)\bm{U}:=w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{4}\left(\frac{1}{1-c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}\mu}\right)\mathscr{J}_{w}\bm{U}.

Then the identity (
3.5.5
) reads

\mathcal{J}_{\mu}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\kappa,w)-\mathcal{J}_{0}(\bm{U};\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\kappa,w)=\mu\mathscr{J}_{*}(\mu;\kappa,w)\bm{U}.

3.6.
The nondegeneracy conditions: verification of Hypothesis
6

3.6.1.
The linear nondegeneracy condition

Put

(3.6.1)

\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,w):=\bm{\chi}_{2}^{*}\big[\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w);\kappa,w\big]-\mathscr{J}_{*}(0;\kappa,w)\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w).

We need to show
\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)>0
.
Using the definition of
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}
in (
3.3.33
) and
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{1}
in (
3.4.3
), it is easy to show that

\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)=0.

Next, the definition of
\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{*}
in (
3.5.8
) implies

\mathscr{J}_{*}(0;\kappa,w)\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)=w(1+\kappa)\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{4}\left(1+\frac{1}{w}\right)<0

since
\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)<0
by its definition in (
3.3.37
) and Remark
3.8
.
Hence

(3.6.2)

\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)=-(1+\kappa)(1+w)\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{4}>0.

3.6.2.
The quadratic nondegeneracy condition

Put

(3.6.3)

\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,w):=2\bm{\chi}_{2}^{*}\big[\mathcal{Q}_{0}\big(\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w),\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w);\kappa,\beta,w\big);\kappa,w\big]-D^{2}\mathcal{J}_{0}(0;\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},\kappa,w)\big[\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w),\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)\big].

We need to show
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,w)\neq 0
.

We recall that
\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}
is defined in (
3.4.4
),
\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{0}
in (
3.5.7
), and
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)
in (
3.3.33
).
It is then straightforward to calculate

\mathcal{Q}_{0}\big(\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w),\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w);\kappa,\beta,w\big)=\frac{(\beta-\kappa^{2})(1+w)}{(1+\kappa)\kappa{w}}\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
-1\\
w\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}.

Next, the definition of
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{2}^{*}[\cdot;\kappa,w]
in (
3.3.41
) gives

(3.6.4)

2\bm{\chi}_{2}^{*}\big[\mathcal{Q}_{0}\big(\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w),\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w);\kappa,\beta,w\big);\kappa,w\big]=-\frac{8(\beta-\kappa^{2})\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)(\kappa-1)w}{(1+\kappa)^{2}},

where
\displaystyle\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)\neq 0
was defined in (
3.3.37
).

Finally, we can calculate
\displaystyle D^{2}\mathcal{J}_{\mu}
using (
3.5.7
) and (
3.5.6
).
For
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},P_{1},P_{2})\in\mathcal{X}
in general, we find

D^{2}\mathcal{J}(0;\mathcal{V}_{1},\mathcal{V}_{2},w,c)[\bm{U},\bm{U}]=4\beta\int_{-1}^{0}P_{1}(s+1)P_{2}(s)\ ds-2\beta\int_{-1}^{0}\big(P_{1}(s+1)^{2}+P_{2}(s)^{2}\big)\ ds\\
+4\int_{0}^{1}P_{1}(s-1)P_{2}(s)\ ds-2\int_{0}^{1}\big(P_{1}(s-1)^{2}+P_{2}(s)^{2}\big)\ ds.

Then we evaluate this second derivative at the generalized eigenvector
\displaystyle\bm{U}=\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)
defined in (
3.3.33
) and rescaling the result per (
3.5.7
) to find

(3.6.5)

D^{2}\mathcal{J}_{0}(0;\kappa,\beta,w)\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w),\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,w)]=-\frac{8(\beta+\kappa^{2})w\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)}{1+\kappa}.

Subtracting the value in
3.6.5
from (
3.6.4
), we conclude

(3.6.6)

\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,w)=\frac{16w\mathsf{a}_{-4}(\kappa,w)(\beta+\kappa^{3})}{(1+\kappa)^{2}}\neq 0,

provided that
\displaystyle\beta+\kappa^{3}\neq 0
.
This, of course, leads to our requirement that
\displaystyle\beta\neq\kappa^{3}
in Theorem
1.5
.

3.7.
Optimal regularity: verification of Hypothesis
7

We presume familiarity here with our nomenclature in Appendix
C
.
The central difficulty of verifying this hypotheses is that the operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
from (
3.3.29
) does not have the good resolvent estimate (
C.6.1
) and so we cannot just apply Theorem
C.6
to conclude the hypothesis.
Indeed, for
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\displaystyle|k|>\omega_{*}(\kappa,w)
, define
\displaystyle\bm{U}^{(k)}=\big(p_{1}^{(k)},p_{2}^{(k)},\xi_{1}^{(k)},\xi_{2}^{(k)},P_{1}^{(k)},P_{2}^{(k)}\big)\in\mathcal{X}
via
\displaystyle p_{1}^{(k)}=1/2
,
\displaystyle p_{2}^{(k)}=\xi_{1}^{(k)}=\xi_{2}^{(k)}=0
,
\displaystyle P_{1}^{(k)}(v)=0
, and
\displaystyle P_{2}^{(k)}(v)=e^{ikv}/2
.
Then with the resolvent
\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(ik;\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))
defined in part
(v)
of Proposition
3.1
, we have

\|\bm{U}^{(k)}\|_{\mathcal{X}}=1\qquad\text{ but }\qquad\lim_{k\to\infty}\|\mathcal{R}(ik;\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))\bm{U}^{(k)}\|_{\mathcal{X}}\neq 0.

Consequently, we must work much more explicitly with the (sub)optimal regularity problems.
Let

\mathcal{Y}:=\!\left\{(0,0,\xi_{1},\xi_{2},0,0)\in\mathcal{X}\ \middle|\ \xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}\right\},

so
\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}\subseteq\mathcal{D}
and also

\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)\bm{U}\in\mathcal{Y}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathcal{Q}(\bm{U},\mu;\kappa,\beta,\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w)\in\mathcal{Y}

for all
\displaystyle\bm{U}\in\mathcal{X}
, per the structure of these operators given in (
3.4.3
) and (
3.4.5
).
Thus if

(3.7.1)

\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}:=(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\mathcal{Y},

then

(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\mathcal{L}_{1}(\kappa,w)\bm{U}\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\mathcal{Q}(\bm{U},\mu;\kappa,\beta,\mathscr{V}_{1},\mathscr{V}_{2},w)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}.

The localized optimal, periodic optimal, and suboptimal regularity problems of Hypothesis
7
all have the following broad structure in their lattice incarnations.
Given a map
\displaystyle\bm{G}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}
and a scalar
\displaystyle\mathsf{m}\in\mathbb{R}
, we need to solve a differential equation of the form

(3.7.2)

\bm{U}^{\prime}(x)=\mathsf{m}\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)\bm{U}(x)+\bm{G}(x),

where
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)
was defined in (
3.3.29
).
Of course, for each kind of problem we will have some more stringent requirements on
\displaystyle\bm{G}
,
\displaystyle\bm{U}
, and
\displaystyle\mathsf{m}
.
In particular, for the localized problem we will need to extend the solution
\displaystyle\bm{U}
from the real line to a small complex strip.
For now, we just sketch the common method of how one starts to solve (
3.7.2
).

Write, as usual,

\bm{U}(x)=\big(p_{1}(x),p_{2}(x),\xi_{1}(x),\xi_{2}(x),P_{1}(x,\cdot),P_{2}(x,\cdot)\big)

and

\bm{G}(x)=\big(g_{1}(x),g_{2}(x),h_{1}(x),h_{2}(x),G_{1}(x,\cdot),G_{2}(x,\cdot)\big).

Then (
3.7.2
) requires
\displaystyle P_{1}
and
\displaystyle P_{2}
to satisfy the forced transport equations

\begin{cases}\partial_{x}[P_{j}]-\mathsf{m}\partial_{v}[P_{j}]=G_{j}\\
P_{j}(x,0)=p_{j}(x).\end{cases}

The unique solution to this problem is

(3.7.3)

P_{j}(x,v)=p_{j}(x+\mathsf{m}^{-1}v)-\mathsf{m}^{-1}\int_{0}^{v}G_{j}(x+\mathsf{m}^{-1}(v-s),s)\ ds.

Abbreviate

\bm{p}:=(p_{1},p_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2}),

\bm{\Delta}_{\mathsf{m}}(\kappa,w)\bm{p}:=\begin{pmatrix}\xi_{1}\\
\xi_{2}\\
-c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}(1+\kappa)p_{1}+c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}(S^{1/\mathsf{m}}+\kappa{S}^{-1/\mathsf{m}})p_{2}\\
-c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}w(1+\kappa)p_{2}+c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}w(\kappa{S}^{1/\mathsf{m}}+S^{-1/\mathsf{m}})p_{1}\end{pmatrix},

\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\mathsf{m}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)(x):=-\frac{1}{c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}\mathsf{m}}\left(\int_{0}^{1}G_{2}(x+\mathsf{m}^{-1}(1-s),s)\ ds-\kappa\int_{-1}^{0}G_{2}(x+\mathsf{m}^{-1}(-1-s),s)\ ds\right),

\mathfrak{R}_{4}^{\mathsf{m}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)(x):=-\frac{w}{c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}\mathsf{m}}\left(\kappa\int_{0}^{1}G_{1}(x+\mathsf{m}^{-1}(1-s),s)\ ds-\int_{-1}^{0}G_{1}(x+\mathsf{m}^{-1}(1-s),s)\ ds\right),

and

\bm{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathsf{m}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w):=\big(g_{1},g_{2},\mathfrak{R}_{3}^{\mathsf{m}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)+h_{1},\mathfrak{R}_{4}^{\mathsf{m}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)+h_{2}\big).

Then our remaining unknowns
\displaystyle\bm{p}
must satisfy

(3.7.4)

\bm{p}^{\prime}=\mathsf{m}\bm{\Delta}_{\mathsf{m}}(\kappa,w)\bm{p}+\bm{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathsf{m}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w).

This is really an advance-delay differential equation, due to the shift operators in
\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}_{\mathsf{m}}(\kappa,w)
.

We sketch in the following sections how we solve (
3.7.4
) for each regularity problem.
We do not provide the full details here.
Our foremost hesitation to do so arises from the burdensome calculation that gives the precise form of
\displaystyle\bm{G}=(\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{\mathcal{X}}-\Pi)\widetilde{\bm{G}}
, where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{G}}=(0,0,h_{1},h_{2},0,0)
.
A glance at the formulas for the coordinate functionals of
\displaystyle\Pi_{0}
alone, as given in Proposition
3.7
, indicates just how detailed this enterprise is.

Given the precise information that we have presented elsewhere, however, a difficult calculation alone is not a reason for omission.
Rather, we contend that, thanks to the detailed work of our predecessors, we would not learn anything truly new by presenting all the details here.
We feel that our
distillation
and
phrasing
of the optimal regularity problems in Appendix
C
is one of our novel contributions to the spatial dynamics community; the ideas behind their
verifications
follow the established lattice literature.
Conversely, the prior detailed calculations of the generalized eigenvectors and nondegeneracy coefficients really are different in the context of our FPUT dimers from any other lattice results, and they are absolutely essential to determining both the precise leading order structure of our position fronts and to comparing our results with the Beale’s method relative displacement nanopterons.

Finally, we believe that the optimal regularity properties of the system (
3.7.4
) could be generalized substantially to similar linear affine problems with
\displaystyle\bm{\Delta}_{\mathsf{m}}(\kappa,w)
replaced by a much more abstract operator of an advance-delay or Fourier multiplier type, along with a suitable substitute for the affine term
\displaystyle\bm{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mathsf{m}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)
.
Working out the right hypotheses to encompass our problem (
3.7.4
), along with the analogues in the lattice papers
[
43
,
7
,
46
,
35
,
41
,
85
]
is far beyond the scope of this project, but we hope to treat it in detail in the future.
Such a treatment would shed further light on the underlying similarities among the generalized Fourier techniques of
[
43
]
and the classical Fourier methods of
[
21
,
16
]
, between which we outline an interpolation in Section
3.7.1
to address the localized problem, and the Laplace transform techniques of
[
38
]
, which we mention in Section
3.7.3
for tackling the suboptimal problem.

3.7.1.
A sketch of the solution to the localized problem

We can show that the optimal regularity hypothesis holds whenever
\displaystyle q\in(0,\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,w)^{1/2})
and
\displaystyle b_{0}\in(0,\pi)
.
Here we assume that
\displaystyle\bm{p}
and
\displaystyle\bm{G}
are exponentially localized, and so they have Fourier transforms.
Also,
\displaystyle\mathsf{m}=\mu
.
On the Fourier side, (
3.7.4
) reads

\big(i\mu{k}\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{4}-\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}(\mu{k};\kappa,w)\big)\widehat{\bm{p}}(k)=\mu\mathfrak{F}[\bm{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mu^{-1}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)](k),

where

\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{4}:=\diag(1,1,1,1)

and, for
\displaystyle K\in\mathbb{R}
,

\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}(K;\kappa,w):=\begin{bmatrix}0&0&1&0\\
0&0&0&1\\
-c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}(1+\kappa)&c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}(e^{iK}+\kappa{e}^{-iK})&0&0\\
c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}w(\kappa{e}^{iK}+e^{-iK})&-c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{-2}w(1+\kappa)&0&0\end{bmatrix}.

We find

(3.7.5)

\det\big(iK\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{4}-\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}(K;\kappa,w)\big)=\Lambda(K;\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w)),

where
\displaystyle\Lambda
was defined in (
3.3.23
).
Part
(i)
of Proposition
3.2
thereby imposes “solvability conditions” on the problem (
3.7.4
): we need

(3.7.6)

\mathfrak{F}[\bm{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mu^{-1}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)](0)=\mathfrak{F}[\bm{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mu^{-1}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)](\pm\mu\omega_{*}(\kappa,w))=0.

Here
\displaystyle\mathfrak{F}[f]
is the Fourier transform of
\displaystyle f
, i.e.,

\mathfrak{F}[f](k):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(x)e^{-ikx}\ dx.

The condition (
3.7.6
) can be met since
\displaystyle\bm{G}(x)\in\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}
, with
\displaystyle\mathcal{Y}_{\mathsf{h}}
from (
3.7.1
).
It is then possible to recover

(3.7.7)

\widehat{\bm{p}}(k)=\mu\big(i\mu{k}\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}_{4}-\widetilde{\bm{\Delta}}(\mu{k};\kappa,w)\big)^{-1}\mathfrak{F}[\bm{\mathfrak{R}}_{\mu^{-1}}(\bm{G};\kappa,w)](k).

Thereafter one defines
\displaystyle\bm{p}
as a convolution; extends, for
\displaystyle\bm{G}
defined on a complex strip, this convolution to the strip; and last obtains many uniform estimates in
\displaystyle\mu
.
The Lombardi-amenable map needed in the definition of localized optimal regularity will be
\displaystyle\mathfrak{M}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{\infty}}
.
Defining the solution as a convolution and then extending that convolution from the real line is essentially what Lombardi does for the water wave problem in
[
62
, Lem. 8.2.1, 8.2.2]
, except his convolutions arise in a more straightforward way from a “quasi-semigroup” generated by the resolvent.

This is essentially the program carried out by Iooss and Kirchgässner in
[
43
, App. 1]
.
They, however, do not incorporate the small parameter
\displaystyle\mu
and, due to their center manifold theory preferences, work in spaces of exponentially
growing
functions.
This motivates their masterful manipulation of Fourier theory in growing spaces, which retains all the familiar properties of the Fourier transform in
\displaystyle L^{p}
-spaces, e.g., with respect to derivatives and convolutions.
See the discussion in Appendix
C.1
for references to subsequent optimal regularity problems treated along the lines of
[
43
]
.

3.7.2.
A sketch of the solution to the periodic problem

The set-up of this problem is largely the same as that in Section
3.7.1
, except now we are working with
\displaystyle 2\pi
-periodic
\displaystyle\bm{p}
and
\displaystyle\bm{G}
, and
\displaystyle\mathsf{m}=(\omega_{*}(\kappa,w)/\mu+\grave{\omega})^{-1}
, where
\displaystyle\grave{\omega}=\mathcal{O}(\mu)
.
In lieu of the Fourier transform, we use Fourier coefficients with
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Again, the crux of the matter is that the determinant of the matrix that we would like to invert is (
3.7.5
), with
\displaystyle K=(\omega/\mu+\grave{\omega})k
, and so we have solvability conditions like (
3.7.7
).
Venney and Zimmer provide an illustrative overview of the problem in their discussion after
[
85
, Eq. (52)]
.

3.7.3.
A sketch of the solution to the suboptimal problem

Now we will allow
\displaystyle\bm{p}
and
\displaystyle\bm{G}
to grow exponentially, and we take
\displaystyle\mathsf{m}=1
.
The system (
3.7.4
) is suited to the Laplace transform-based techniques of Hupkes and Verduyn Lunel for linear nonhomogeneous mixed-type functional differential equations (MFDE); see
[
38
, Sec. 5, Prop. 5.1]
.
Although the generalized Fourier methods of Iooss and Kirchgässner also apply to this growing problem, our prior success in adapting the Hupkes–Verduyn Lunel methods to the FPUT equal mass limit in
[
19
]
leads us to favor the latter approach.
The MFDE approach is also mentioned as a alternative strategy in
[
41
, Sec. II.B]
.

3.8.
Spatial dynamics in relative displacement coordinates

It is wholly possible to make the Iooss–Kirchgässner change of variables from Section
3.1
on the relative displacement traveling wave system (
1.2.4
).
For simplicity, here we assume that the spring forces have no superquadratic terms, i.e.,
\displaystyle\mathscr{V}_{1}=\mathscr{V}_{2}=0
in (
1.1.9
).
Put
\displaystyle\xi_{j}:=\varrho_{j}^{\prime}
and
\displaystyle\Rho_{j}(x,v):=\varrho_{j}(x+v)
.
Doing so yields a problem like (
3.1.7
), which we write in the intentionally similar notation

(3.8.1)

\bm{U}^{\prime}=\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c)\bm{U}+c^{-2}\mathscr{Q}(\bm{U};\beta,w),

where
\displaystyle\bm{U}=(\varrho_{1},\varrho_{2},\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\Rho_{1},\Rho_{2})
, and the operators above are

\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c):=\begin{bmatrix}0&\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}&0\\
-(1+w)c^{-2}\diag(1,\kappa)&0&c^{-2}\Delta^{\star}(\kappa,w)\\
0&0&\partial_{v}\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}\end{bmatrix},

\Delta^{\star}(\kappa,w):=\begin{bmatrix}0&\kappa(w\delta^{1}+\delta^{-1})\\
(\delta^{1}+w\delta^{-1})&0\end{bmatrix},

and

\mathscr{Q}(\bm{U};\beta,w):=\begin{pmatrix}0\\
0\\
-(1+w)\varrho_{1}^{2}+\beta(w\delta^{1}+\delta^{-1})\Rho_{2}^{2}\\
-\beta(1+w)\varrho_{2}^{2}+(\delta^{1}+w\delta^{-1})\Rho_{1}^{2}\\
0\\
0\end{pmatrix}

This problem is reversible in the mass and spring dimer cases with the respective symmetries

\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{M}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{J}&0&0\\
0&-\mathbb{J}&0\\
0&0&R\mathbb{J}\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{K}}:=\begin{bmatrix}\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}&0&0\\
0&-\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}&0\\
0&0&R\text{\scalebox{1.15}{$\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}$}}\end{bmatrix}

with
\displaystyle R
defined in (
3.2.3
),
\displaystyle\mathbb{J}
defined in (
3.2.5
), and

\displaystyle\mathbbold{1}

defined in (
3.1.10
).
It is also possible to construct a first integral very much like the one in (
3.5.3
).

An analysis exactly along the lines of Section
3.3.1
shows that
\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c)
has the same spectrum as
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
, the linearization of the position problem.
That is,

\sigma(\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c))=\!\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}\ \middle|\ \det\big(\mathcal{M}(z;\kappa,w,c)\big)=0\right\},

with the determinant calculated in (
3.3.9
).
Moreover, when
\displaystyle c=c_{*}(\kappa,w)
from (
3.3.24
), the center spectrum of
\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))
is the same; it consists of the eigenvalues 0 and
\displaystyle\pm{i}\omega_{c}(\kappa,w)
, as in Proposition
3.2
.
These eigenvalues have the same algebraic and geometric multiplicities as in that proposition, too.

This should not be surprising.
At the
linear
level, the position and relative displacement traveling wave problems are closely related.
The linear operators governing (
1.2.2
) and (
1.2.4
) are, respectively,

\begin{bmatrix}-(1+\kappa)&(S^{1}+\kappa{S}^{-1})\\
w(\kappa{S}^{1}+S^{-1})&-w(1+\kappa)\end{bmatrix}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\begin{bmatrix}-(1+w)&\kappa(wS^{1}+S^{-1})\\
(S^{1}+wS^{-1})&-\kappa(1+w)\end{bmatrix}.

We can view these operators as formal adjoints of each other, provided that the parameters
\displaystyle\kappa
and
\displaystyle w
are swapped.
In particular, if we consider them as Fourier multipliers, their symbols are the conjugate transposes of each other, albeit with
\displaystyle\kappa
and
\displaystyle w
interchanged.
This “formal adjoint” structure is certainly replicated in the components of
\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c)
as compared to those of
\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\kappa,w,c)
in (
3.1.10
).

Because 0 has algebraic multiplicity 4 as an eigenvalue of
\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c_{*}(\kappa,w))
, we cannot, as in position coordinates, directly apply Lombardi’s results to (
3.8.1
).
The obstacle to a reduction procedure like that of Section
2
appears to be that (
3.8.1
) is no longer translation invariant; this is a divergence of (
3.8.1
) and the position problem (
3.1.7
) at the
nonlinear
level.
More precisely, while the one-dimensional eigenspace of
\displaystyle\mathscr{L}(\kappa,w,c)
corresponding to 0 is spanned by the eigenvector

\bm{E}_{0}(\kappa,w):=(\kappa,1,0,0,\kappa,1),

in general we have

\mathscr{Q}(\bm{U}+\mu\bm{E}_{0}(\kappa,w);\beta,w)\neq\mathscr{Q}(\bm{U};\beta,w)

Physically, the loss of translation invariance is unsurprising, as the original relative displacement problem (
1.1.6
) does not retain the affine invariance that the position problem does, as we outlined in Remark
3.6
.

Additionally, in the separate mass and spring dimer cases, we have

(3.8.2)

\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{M}}\bm{E}_{0}(1,w)=\bm{E}_{0}(1,w)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathscr{S}_{\mathbf{K}}\bm{E}_{0}(\kappa,1)=\bm{E}_{0}(\kappa,1),

and so (
3.8.1
) has a “
\displaystyle 0^{4+}i\omega
” bifurcation at the origin, which contrasts with the
\displaystyle 0^{4-}i\omega
bifurcation in position coordinates; see Remark
2.2
.
Given these subtle, but substantial, distinctions between the position and relative displacement problems, and considering our success with running spatial dynamics in position coordinates, we do not pursue further an attempt at spatial dynamics in relative displacement coordinates.
Neither, however, are we willing to rule out the possibility that Lombardi’s methods could apply to some reduced version of (
3.8.1
) as they do for position.

4.
The Proofs of Theorems
1.4
and
1.5

We prove Theorem
1.4
in detail in Section
4.1
.
The proof of Theorem
1.5
, which we give in Section
4.2
, will then be quite similar, so we only discuss the notable points of departure.
These chiefly hinge on the very different structures of the generalized eigenvectors
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}(1,w)
and
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,1)
, which can be seen from their definitions in (
3.3.33
).

Since the mass and spring dimers separately satisfy Hypotheses
1
through
7
, Theorem
2.4
provides solutions to the problem (
3.4.6
) when
\displaystyle\kappa=\beta=1
or when
\displaystyle w=1
.
These solutions are given in the Iooss–Kirchgässner variables
\displaystyle\bm{U}
, as defined in Section
3.1
, and so our main task is to convert them back to position and relative displacement coordinates.
The recovery of position coordinates is fairly straightforward, but the relative displacement construction requires rather more detail.
In particular, we put significant effort into establishing that the periodic profiles for relative displacement cannot vanish identically and into extracting just the right explicit and abstract exponentially localized terms in the nanopteron profile, so that our results match those from Beale’s method in
[
21
,
16
]
.

4.1.
The proof of Theorem
1.4

4.1.1.
Solutions to the near-sonic traveling wave problem (
3.4.6
) in Iooss–Kirchgässner variables

The work in Section
3
shows that the near-sonic traveling wave problem (
3.4.6
) satisfies Hypotheses
1
through
7
, and therefore by Theorem
2.4
this problem has solutions
\displaystyle\bm{U}=\mathsf{U}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle\bm{U}=\mathsf{P}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
, where
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
has the form (
2.2.2
) and
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\mu}^{\alpha}
has the form (
2.2.8
).
Here
\displaystyle 0<\mu<\mu_{*}
.
To bring the small parameter in line with the classical long wave scaling, we take
\displaystyle\mu=\epsilon^{2}
and restrict
\displaystyle 0<\epsilon<\mu_{*}^{1/2}
.

With
\displaystyle\mu=\epsilon^{2}
, our solutions are

(4.1.1)

\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(x)=\epsilon\left[\left(-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}+\epsilon\Lup_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}\right)\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}\epsilon{x}}{2}\right)+\epsilon\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}(\epsilon{x})\right]\bm{\chi}_{0}(1,w)\\
-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}\epsilon^{2}\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}\epsilon{x}}{2}\right)\bm{\chi}_{1}(1,w)+\epsilon^{3}\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}(\epsilon{x})\\
+\alpha\epsilon^{2}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(\Tup_{\epsilon^{2}}(\epsilon{x}))+\alpha\epsilon(\alpha+\epsilon)\left(\int_{0}^{\Tup_{\epsilon^{2}}(\epsilon{x})}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds\right)\bm{\chi}_{0}(1,w)

and

(4.1.2)

\mathsf{P}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(x)=\alpha\epsilon^{2}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(\epsilon{x})+\alpha\epsilon(\alpha+\epsilon^{2})\left(\int_{0}^{\epsilon{x}}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds\right)\bm{\chi}_{0}(1,w)

with

\Tup_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(X):=X+\epsilon^{2}\vartheta_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}X}{2}\right).

The eigenvectors
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{0}(1,w)
and
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}(1,w)
were defined in (
3.3.32
) and (
3.3.33
), respectively.
The maps
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}
,
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}
,
\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
, and
\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}
and the scalars
\displaystyle\Lup_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle\vartheta_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
have all the same properties as their counterparts in Theorem
2.4
, using the identity
\displaystyle\mu=\epsilon^{2}
throughout.
The scalar
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)
was computed in (
3.6.6
).
To eliminate unnecessary instances of
\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}
, put

(4.1.3)

\theta_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}:=\vartheta_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha},\qquad\mathsf{L}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}:=\Lup_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}:=\Tup_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}.

We conclude this introduction by selecting the values of
\displaystyle b_{0}
and
\displaystyle q
in the statement of Theorem
2.4
.
We fix an arbitrary
\displaystyle q\in(0,\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2})
, where
\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)
was defined in (
3.6.1
).
Purely for simplicity, we take
\displaystyle b_{0}=\pi/2
and set

(4.1.4)

\Alpha_{\infty}:=\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\omega_{*}(1,w)\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2},

where
\displaystyle\omega_{*}(1,w)
was defined in (
3.3.29
).
Theorem
2.4
then tells us that the solutions
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
exist over the ranges

(4.1.5)

0<\epsilon<\widetilde{\epsilon}_{*}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Alpha_{0}\epsilon{e}^{-\Alpha_{\infty}/\epsilon}\leq\alpha\leq\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1}

for some
\displaystyle\Alpha_{0}
,
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1}
,
\displaystyle\widetilde{\epsilon}_{*}>0
.

4.1.2.
The proof of part
(i)
of Theorem
1.4

With
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
defined in (
4.1.2
), put

(4.1.6)

p_{\per,j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x):=(\mathsf{P}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(x))_{j},\ j=1,2,

so that by the Iooss–Kirchgässner change of variables in Section
3.1
, the pair
\displaystyle(p_{\per,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha},p_{\per,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha})
solves the position traveling wave problem (
1.2.2
).
Define

(4.1.7)

\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\begin{cases}(\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(X))_{1},\ j\text{ is odd}\\
(\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(X))_{2},\ j\text{ is even}\end{cases}

and

(4.1.8)

\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\int_{0}^{X}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds.

The estimate
2.2.6
from Theorem
2.4
guarantees that these function satisfy the estimate (
1.5.4
) in Theorem
1.4
.
By (
D.1.7
), the leading order term of the frequency of
\displaystyle\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
is
\displaystyle\omega_{w}:=\omega_{*}(1,w)
, which was defined in (
3.3.29
).

We then have

(4.1.9)

p_{\per,j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)=\alpha\epsilon^{2}\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon{x})+\alpha\epsilon(\alpha+\epsilon^{2})\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon{x}),

and so taking

u_{j}(t)=\begin{cases}p_{\per,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-c_{\epsilon}{t}),\ j\text{ is odd}\\
p_{\per,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-c_{\epsilon}{t}),\ j\text{ is even}\end{cases}

produces the solutions (
1.5.3
) to the position system (
1.1.4
).

4.1.3.
The proof of part
(ii)
of Theorem
1.4

With
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
defined in (
4.1.1
), put

(4.1.10)

p_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)=(\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(x))_{j},\ j=1,2.

By the Iooss–Kirchgässner change of variables, this solves the position traveling wave profile system (
1.2.2
), and thus taking

u_{j}(t)=\begin{cases}p_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-\mathsf{c}_{\epsilon^{2}}(1,w){t}),\ j\text{ is odd}\\
p_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-\mathsf{c}_{\epsilon^{2}}(1,w){t}),\ j\text{ is even}\end{cases}

solves the original equations of motion (
1.1.4
).
The wave speed
\displaystyle\mathsf{c}_{\epsilon^{2}}(1,w)
was defined in (
3.4.1
).

Now we rewrite these solutions into the form promised by part
(ii)
of Theorem
1.4
.
First, we will need the identities

(4.1.11)

(\bm{\chi}_{0}(1,w))_{1}=(\bm{\chi}_{0}(1,w))_{2}=1\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(\bm{\chi}_{1}(1,w))_{1}=(\bm{\chi}_{1}(1,w))_{2}=0.

Here we are using the componentwise notation from (
3.1.5
).
Next, abbreviate

(4.1.12)

\eta_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\begin{cases}\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}(X)+\epsilon(\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}(X))_{1},\ j\text{ is odd}\\
\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}(X)+\epsilon(\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}(X))_{2},\ j\text{ is even}.\end{cases}

The estimates
2.2.4
from Theorem
2.4
then guarantee that these three functions satisfy the estimate (
1.5.8
) of Theorem
1.4
.

Last, define

(4.1.13)

\mathsf{F}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\left(-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}+\epsilon\mathsf{L}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}\right)\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}X}{2}\right)+\epsilon\eta_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)+\alpha\epsilon\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X))\\
+\alpha(\alpha+\epsilon^{2})\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)),

where
\displaystyle\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
was defined in (
4.1.7
) and
\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}
in (
4.1.8
).
We conclude that
\displaystyle p_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)=\epsilon\mathsf{F}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon{x})
and thus
\displaystyle u_{j}(t)=\epsilon\mathsf{F}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon(j-c_{\epsilon}{t}))
.
Using the definition of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)
in (
3.6.2
) and the definition of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)
in (
3.6.6
), we calculate

-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}=\left(\frac{6w(w^{2}-w+1)}{(1+w)^{3}}\right)^{1/2}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}}{2}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{6w(1+w)}{w^{2}-w+1}\right)^{1/2}.

This leads to the identities (
1.5.6
) and (
1.5.7
).

4.1.4.
The proof of part
(iii)
of Theorem
1.4

With
\displaystyle p_{\per,j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
defined in (
4.1.9
), the identities (
1.2.5
) tell us that the pair
\displaystyle(\varrho_{\per,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha},\varrho_{\per,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha})
defined by

(4.1.14)

\varrho_{\per,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x):=p_{\per,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x+1)-p_{\per,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\varrho_{\per,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x):=p_{\per,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x+1)-p_{\per,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)

solves the relative displacement traveling wave profile system (
1.2.4
).
Consequently, taking

r_{j}(t)=\begin{cases}\varrho_{\per,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-\mathsf{c}_{\epsilon^{2}}(1,w){t}),\ j\text{ is odd}\\
\varrho_{\per,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-\mathsf{c}_{\epsilon^{2}}(1,w){t}),\ j\text{ is even}\end{cases}

solves the original traveling wave problem.

Now we rewrite these solutions into the form (
1.5.9
) stated in Theorem
1.4
.
The definitions of
\displaystyle\varrho_{\per,j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle p_{\per,j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
motivate us to set

(4.1.15)

\widetilde{\varphi}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\begin{cases}[\varphi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon)-\varphi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)]+\epsilon^{-1}(\alpha+\epsilon^{2})\int_{X}^{X+\epsilon}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds,\ j\text{ is odd}\\[10.0pt]
[\varphi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon)-\varphi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)]+\epsilon^{-1}(\alpha+\epsilon^{2})\int_{X}^{X+\epsilon}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds,\ j\text{ is even},\end{cases}

where
\displaystyle\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
was defined in (
4.1.7
).
Then

\varrho_{\per,j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)=\alpha\epsilon^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon{x}),\ j=1,2.

and so putting

r_{j}(t)=\alpha\epsilon^{2}\widetilde{\varphi}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon(j-c_{\epsilon}{t})),

solves the relative displacement problem (
1.1.6
).

It follows immediately from (
4.1.15
) that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
is periodic in
\displaystyle X
with periodic independent of
\displaystyle j
and uniformly bounded in
\displaystyle\alpha
,
\displaystyle\epsilon
, and
\displaystyle X
.
Last, we verify that
\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
is not identically zero; the proof for
\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
is the same.
Our strategy is to write
\displaystyle\varphi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\cdot+\epsilon)-\varphi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
as the sum of a leading order function whose value at 0 is nonzero and uniformly bounded away from 0 and a “higher-order” function.
Then we use the fact that the integral terms in
\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
come with prefactors of
\displaystyle\alpha
and
\displaystyle\epsilon
that force them to be uniformly small, at least if the ranges of
\displaystyle\alpha
and
\displaystyle\epsilon
are suitably controlled.

By (
D.1.6
), (
D.1.7
), and (
D.1.8
), we can expand

(4.1.16)

\frac{\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)}{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)}=\cos(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}X)\left(\frac{(\bm{\chi}_{\omega}(1,w))_{j}+(\bm{\chi}_{-\omega}(1,w))_{j}}{2}\right)\\
+\sin(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}X)\left(\frac{(\bm{\chi}_{\omega}(1,w))_{j}-(\bm{\chi}_{-\omega}(1,w))_{j}}{2i}\right)+\alpha\psi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}X),

where

(4.1.17)

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\epsilon<\widetilde{\epsilon}_{*}\\
0\leq\alpha\leq\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1}\\
X\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}|\psi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)|<\infty

and

(4.1.18)

\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}=\frac{\omega_{*}(1,w)}{\epsilon}+\epsilon\widetilde{\Omega}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha},\qquad\text{ with }\qquad\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\epsilon<\widetilde{\epsilon}_{*}\\
0\leq\alpha\leq\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1}\\
X\in\mathbb{R}\end{subarray}}|\widetilde{\Omega}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}|<\infty.

The vectors
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{\pm\omega}(1,w)
were defined in (
3.3.47
), while the scalar
\displaystyle\omega_{*}(1,w)
was defined in (
3.3.29
).

Put

(4.1.19)

\mathsf{E}_{w}:=\frac{\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))}{1-\dfrac{w}{1+w}(\omega_{*}(1,w))^{2}}.

Then the definition of
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{\pm\omega}(1,w)
in (
3.3.47
), we find

\frac{\bm{\chi}_{\omega}(1,w))_{j}+(\bm{\chi}_{-\omega}(1,w))_{j}}{2}=\begin{cases}\mathsf{E}_{w},\ j=1\\
1,\ j=2\end{cases}

and

\frac{\bm{\chi}_{\omega}(1,w))_{j}-(\bm{\chi}_{-\omega}(1,w))_{j}}{2i}=0,\ j=1,2.

Abbreviate

f_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\frac{\varphi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon)-\varphi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)}{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}}

to find

(4.1.20)

f_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)=\cos(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon))-\mathsf{E}_{w}\cos(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}X)+\alpha(\psi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon))-\psi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}X)).

We consider two cases.

Case 1.

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{w}\neq 0
.
Then, per (
4.1.19
),
\displaystyle\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))\neq 0
.
We claim for the moment that
\displaystyle\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))-\mathsf{E}_{w}\neq 0
and we calculate

f_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(0)=\cos(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}\epsilon)-\mathsf{E}_{w}+\alpha(\psi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}\epsilon)-\psi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(0))\\
=\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w)+\epsilon^{2}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha})-\mathsf{E}_{w}+\alpha(\psi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\omega_{*}(1,w)+\epsilon^{2}\widetilde{\Omega}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha})-\psi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(0)).

We use the uniform bounds on
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Omega}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}
and
\displaystyle\psi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
to find
\displaystyle\breve{\epsilon}_{*}\in(0,\widetilde{\epsilon}_{*})
and
\displaystyle\breve{\Alpha}_{1}\in(0,\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1})
such that if
\displaystyle 0<\epsilon<\breve{\epsilon}_{*}
, then
\displaystyle\Alpha_{0}\epsilon{e}^{-\Alpha_{\infty}/\epsilon}<\breve{\Alpha}_{1}
and if
\displaystyle\Alpha_{0}\epsilon{e}^{-\Alpha_{\infty}/\epsilon}\leq\alpha\leq\breve{\Alpha}_{1}
, then

|f_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(0)|\geq\frac{|\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))-\mathsf{E}_{w}|}{2}>0.

Then we use the uniform bound (
2.2.6
) on
\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\int,\alpha}
to find
\displaystyle\epsilon_{*}\in(0,\breve{\epsilon}_{*})
and
\displaystyle\Alpha_{1}\in(0,\breve{\Alpha}_{1})
such that if
\displaystyle 0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{*}
, then
\displaystyle\Alpha_{0}\epsilon{e}^{-\Alpha_{\infty}/\epsilon}<\Alpha_{1}
and

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{*}\\
0\alpha\leq\Alpha_{1}\end{subarray}}|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(0)|\geq\frac{|\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))-\mathsf{E}_{w}|}{4}>0.

Finally, we check that
\displaystyle\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))-\mathsf{E}_{w}\neq 0
as claimed above.
Otherwise, if
\displaystyle\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))-\mathsf{E}_{w}=0
, then the definition of
\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{w}
in (
4.1.19
) gives

1-\frac{1}{1-\dfrac{w}{1+w}(\omega_{*}(1,w))^{2}}=0.

But this rearranges to
\displaystyle\omega_{*}(1,w)=0
, which is impossible by part
(i)
of Proposition
3.2
, so
\displaystyle\cos(\omega_{*}(1,w))-\mathsf{E}_{w}\neq 0
.

Case 2.

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{w}=0
.
Then from (
4.1.20
) we calculate

f_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(-\epsilon)=1+\alpha(\psi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(0)-\psi_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(-\Omega_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}\epsilon)),

and exactly the same type of restrictions on
\displaystyle\epsilon
and
\displaystyle\alpha
as above guarantee that for some
\displaystyle\epsilon_{*}\in(0,\epsilon_{0})
and
\displaystyle\Alpha_{1}\in(0,\widetilde{\Alpha}_{1})
we have

\inf_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{*}\\
0\leq\alpha\leq\Alpha_{1}\end{subarray}}|\widetilde{\varphi}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(-\epsilon)|\geq\frac{1}{2}.

4.1.5.
The proof of part
(iv)
of Theorem
1.4

With
\displaystyle p_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
defined in (
4.1.10
), put

(4.1.21)

\varrho_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x):=p_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x+1)-p_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\varrho_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x):=p_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x+1)-p_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)

to see that the pair
\displaystyle(\varrho_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha},\varrho_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha})
solves the relative displacement traveling wave profile system (
1.2.4
).
Thus taking

(4.1.22)

r_{j}(t)=\begin{cases}\varrho_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-c_{\epsilon}{t}),\ j\text{ is odd}\\
\varrho_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(j-c_{\epsilon}{t}),\ j\text{ is even}\end{cases}

solves the original equations of motion (
1.1.6
) in relative displacement coordinates.

Now we rewrite these solutions into the nanopteron form of part
(iv)
in Theorem
1.4
.
Since
\displaystyle p_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(x)=\epsilon\mathsf{F}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon{x})
, we are led to put

(4.1.23)

\mathsf{N}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\begin{cases}\epsilon^{-1}[\mathsf{F}_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon)-\mathsf{F}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)],\ j\text{ is odd}\\
\epsilon^{-1}[\mathsf{F}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon)-\mathsf{F}_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)],\ j\text{ is even}\end{cases}

and unravel the definitions (
4.1.21
) and (
4.1.22
) to see that defining
\displaystyle r_{j}
by (
4.1.22
) implies

(4.1.24)

r_{j}(t)=\epsilon^{2}\mathsf{N}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon(j-c_{\epsilon}{t})).

We just need to show that
\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
has the nanopteron form (
1.5.11
).
We only do this for
\displaystyle j
odd, as the work for
\displaystyle j
even is identical.

We use the definitions of
\displaystyle\mathsf{N}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
in (
4.1.23
) and
\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
in (
4.1.13
) to write

(4.1.25)

\mathsf{N}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)=-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}X}{2}\right)+\epsilon\widetilde{\eta}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)+\alpha\widetilde{\varphi}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)),

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\varphi}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
was defined in (
4.1.15
) and where we put

(4.1.26)

\widetilde{\eta}_{1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}:=\sum_{k=1}^{6}\widetilde{\eta}_{1,k,\epsilon}^{\alpha},

with

\widetilde{\eta}_{1,1,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=-\epsilon^{-2}\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}\left[\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}(X+\epsilon)}{2}\right)-\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}X}{2}\right)\right]\\
+\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}X}{2}\right),

\widetilde{\eta}_{1,2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\epsilon^{-1}\mathsf{L}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}\left[\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}(X+\epsilon)}{2}\right)-\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)^{1/2}X}{2}\right)\right],

\widetilde{\eta}_{1,3,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\epsilon^{-1}[\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}(X+\epsilon)-\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}(X)],

\widetilde{\eta}_{1,4,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=(\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}(X+\epsilon))_{2}-(\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}(X))_{1},

\widetilde{\eta}_{1,5,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\alpha(\alpha+\epsilon^{2})\epsilon^{-2}\left(\int_{\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)}^{\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon)}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds-\int_{\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)}^{\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)+\epsilon}\Phi_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,\int}(s)\ ds\right),

and

\widetilde{\eta}_{1,6,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X):=\alpha\epsilon^{-1}[\varphi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X+\epsilon))-\varphi_{2,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)+\epsilon)].

We emphasize that the leading-order
\displaystyle\sech^{2}
-term in (
4.1.25
) is the same as the coefficient on
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}(1,w)
in (
4.1.1
), except for the now missing factor of
\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}
.
While the zero components of
\displaystyle\bm{\chi}_{1}(1,w)
eliminate this
\displaystyle\sech^{2}
-term in the position profiles (
4.1.13
), it reappears here due to the subtraction of the
\displaystyle\epsilon
-shifted
\displaystyle\tanh
-terms.

We recall that the terms
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}
and
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}
appeared in the expansion (
4.1.1
) of our original solution
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
and satisfy the same properties as their counterparts in Theorem
2.4
.
We need to show

\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}0<\epsilon<\epsilon_{*}\\
\Alpha_{0}\epsilon{e}^{-\Alpha_{\infty}/\epsilon}\leq\alpha\leq\Alpha_{1}\end{subarray}}e^{q|X|}|\widetilde{\eta}_{1,k,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)|<\infty,\ k=1,\ldots,6.

For
\displaystyle k=1
,
\displaystyle 2
, this follows from Taylor’s theorem.
For
\displaystyle k=3
, we use the estimate (
2.2.4
) on the derivative of
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}
to bound

|\widetilde{\eta}_{1,3,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)|\leq\epsilon^{-1}\int_{X}^{X+\epsilon}|\partial_{s}[\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}](s)|\ ds\leq C\epsilon^{-1}\int_{X}^{X+\epsilon}e^{-q|s|}\ ds\leq Ce^{-q|X|}.

The estimate for
\displaystyle k=4
also follows from (
2.2.4
) applied to the components of
\displaystyle\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}
.
The estimate for
\displaystyle k=5
follows from part
(i)
of Lemma
B.3
and for
\displaystyle k=6
from part
(ii)
of Lemma
B.4
.

Last, the definitions of
\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)
in (
3.6.2
) and
\displaystyle\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)
in (
3.6.6
) give

-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(1,w)}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(1,1,w)}=\frac{3w}{1+w}.

4.2.
The proof of Theorem
1.5

As we indicated earlier, we just point out the few ways in which the spring dimer proof differs from the mass dimer proof given above.
First, instead of defining the solution
\displaystyle\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
via (
4.1.1
), we have

(4.2.1)

\mathsf{U}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(x)=\epsilon\left[\left(-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)^{1/2}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,1)}+\epsilon\Lup_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}\right)\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)^{1/2}\epsilon{x}}{2}\right)+\epsilon\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}(\epsilon{x})\right]\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,1)\\
-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,1)}\epsilon^{2}\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)^{1/2}\epsilon{x}}{2}\right)\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,1)+\epsilon^{3}\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}(\epsilon{x})+\mathsf{P}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(\epsilon^{-1}\Tup_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}(\epsilon{x})).

Here
\displaystyle\mathsf{P}_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha}
has the same form as (
4.1.2
).
The key difference now is that instead of generalized eigenvector component identities (
4.1.11
), we have

(\bm{\chi}_{1}(\kappa,1))_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(\bm{\chi}_{0}(\kappa,1))_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{1-\kappa}{1+\kappa}\right).

This only requires one adjustment to the construction of position solutions: instead of defining the localized term
\displaystyle\eta_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
via (
4.1.12
), include an additional
\displaystyle\sech^{2}
-term:

(4.2.2)

\eta_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)=(-1)^{j}\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)(\kappa-1)}{2\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,1)(1+\kappa)}\sech^{2}\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)^{1/2}X}{2}\right)+\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,0}(X)+\epsilon(\Upsilon_{\epsilon^{2}}^{\alpha,*}(X))_{j}.

With
\displaystyle\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
defined as in (
4.1.7
) and
\displaystyle\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}
as in (
4.1.8
), and using the notation of (
4.1.3
), we find that the spring dimer position solutions
\displaystyle u_{j}
satisfy
\displaystyle u_{j}(t)=\epsilon\mathsf{F}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\epsilon(j-c_{\epsilon}{t}))
, where

\mathsf{F}_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)=\left(-\frac{3\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)^{1/2}}{\mathfrak{Q}_{0}(\kappa,\beta,1)}+\epsilon\mathsf{L}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}\right)\tanh\left(\frac{\mathfrak{L}_{0}(\kappa,1)^{1/2}\epsilon{x}}{2}\right)+\epsilon\eta_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)+\alpha\epsilon\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X))\\
+\alpha(\alpha+\epsilon^{2})\mathsf{G}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(\mathsf{T}_{\epsilon}^{\alpha}(X)).

The leading order frequency term of
\displaystyle\varphi_{j,\epsilon}^{\alpha}
is
\displaystyle\omega_{\kappa}:=\omega_{*}(\kappa,1)
from (
3.3.29
).
We use the formula (
3.6.2
) for
