, Eq. (7.17), Prop. 7.4.8]
, whether
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{1}
vanishes is not at all obvious.
As Lombardi himself writes, “we have a generic non existence [
sic
] result, but from a theoretical point of view, we are not able in general to determine whether [the coefficient
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{1}
] vanishes or not”
[
62
, Rem. 7.1.17]
.

Lombardi expects, but does not prove, that “generically”
\displaystyle\mathfrak{I}_{1}\neq 0
.
The adverb “generically” refers to the original nonlinear operator
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}
from (
D.1.1
) governing the whole problem.
It is not precisely clear how to interpret “generically” when referring to a fairly arbitrary operator.
One possibility, suggested by the language in
[
62
, Rem. 8.5.1]
,
[
46
, Sec. 5.3.1]
,
[
75
, Sec. 6.3.1]
is that if
\displaystyle\mathcal{G}
depends on a fixed parameter — for example, the Bond number in the water wave problem, or the mass ratio
\displaystyle w
in the mass dimer long wave problem — then for almost all values of that parameter, there are no localized solutions, at least when the problems natural small parameter is sufficiently small.

Appendix E
Beale’s Nanopteron Method

As we discussed in Section
1.3
, the papers
[
21
,
16
]
use a functional-analytic method originally due to Beale
[
3
]
to construct the mass and spring dimer nanopterons in relative displacement coordinates.
Here we sketch this method and point out how certain particular steps relate to our spatial dynamics program.
For simplicity, as in Section
3.8
, we assume that the spring forces contain linear and quadratic terms only, so
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{1}(r)=r+r^{2}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}(r)=\kappa{r}+\beta{r}^{2}
.
Then the original relative displacement traveling wave problem (
1.2.4
) becomes

(E.0.1)

\begin{cases}c^{2}\varrho_{1}^{\prime\prime}=-(1+w)\varrho_{1}+\kappa(wS^{1}+S^{-1})\varrho_{2}-(1+w)\varrho_{1}^{2}+\beta(wS^{1}+S^{-1})\varrho_{2}^{2}\\
c^{2}\varrho_{2}^{\prime\prime}=(S^{1}+wS^{-1})\varrho_{1}-\kappa(1+w)\varrho_{2}+(S^{1}+wS^{-1})\varrho_{1}^{2}-\beta(1+w)\varrho_{2}^{2}.\end{cases}

E.1.
Diagonalization

The problem (
E.0.1
) is not stated in the ideal coordinates for Beale’s method.
First consider the linear operator

(E.1.1)

L:=\begin{bmatrix}-(1+w)&\kappa(wS^{1}+S^{-1})\\
(S^{1}+wS^{-1})&-\kappa(1+w)\end{bmatrix}

as a Fourier multiplier with a matrix-valued symbol.
That is, for each
\displaystyle k\in\mathbb{R}
, there is a matrix
\displaystyle\widetilde{L}(k)\in\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
such that

L[e^{ik\cdot}\mathbf{v}](x)=e^{ikx}\widetilde{L}(k)\mathbf{v},\ x\in\mathbb{R},\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{C}^{2}.

The eigenvalues of
\displaystyle\widetilde{L}(k)
are the distinct numbers
\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{\pm}(k)
from (
3.3.26
), so
\displaystyle\widetilde{L}(k)
is diagonalizable.
More precisely, we can write

(E.1.2)

\widetilde{L}(k)=\widetilde{J}(k)\begin{bmatrix}\widetilde{\lambda}_{-}(k)&0\\
0&\widetilde{\lambda}_{+}(k)\end{bmatrix}\widetilde{J}(k)^{-1},

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{J}\colon\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
is
\displaystyle 2\pi
-periodic and real analytic.
The columns of
\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(k)
consist of eigenvectors of
\displaystyle\widetilde{L}(k)
, and so there are many such
\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(k)
that we might use; in the mass and spring dimer cases, we choose particular scalings of the eigenvectors to preserve the parities of the traveling wave profiles later.
This is one instance of the use of (the different) symmetries for mass and spring dimers.

A careful examination of the formula for
\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{-}
in (
3.3.26
) allows us to factor

\widetilde{\lambda}_{-}(k)=2(1-\cos(k))\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-}(k),

where
\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-}
is
\displaystyle 2\pi
-periodic and real analytic.
Likewise,
\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{+}
is
\displaystyle 2\pi
-periodic and real analytic, and so we have the Fourier series expansions

\widetilde{J}(k)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}e^{ikn}J_{n},\qquad\widetilde{\Lambda}_{-}(k)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}^{-}e^{ikn},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\widetilde{\lambda}_{+}(k)=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}^{+}e^{ikn}.

Here
\displaystyle J_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{2\times 2}
and
\displaystyle c_{n}^{\pm}\in\mathbb{C}
.
Since
\displaystyle k\mapsto e^{ikn}
is the symbol of the shift operator
\displaystyle S^{n}
, we are motivated to define

J:=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}S^{n}J_{n},\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Lambda_{\pm}:=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}c_{n}^{\pm}S^{n}

as bounded operators on
\displaystyle L^{\infty}\times L^{\infty}
and
\displaystyle L^{\infty}
, respectively.
Since
\displaystyle k\mapsto 2(1-\cos(k))
is the symbol of
\displaystyle-(S^{1}-2+S^{-1})
, it follows that

L=J\begin{bmatrix}-(S^{1}-2+S^{-1})\Lambda_{-}&0\\
0&\Lambda_{+}\end{bmatrix}J^{-1}.

Now we are ready to diagonalize.
Put

\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{p}_{1}\\
\mathfrak{p}_{2}\end{pmatrix}=J\begin{pmatrix}\varrho_{1}\\
\varrho_{2}\end{pmatrix}

to see that, if the profiles
\displaystyle\varrho_{1}
and
\displaystyle\varrho_{2}
are assumed to be differentiable and bounded, then (
1.2.4
) is equivalent to

(E.1.3)

\begin{cases}c^{2}\mathfrak{p}_{1}^{\prime\prime}-(S^{1}-2+S^{-1})\Lambda_{-}[\mathfrak{p}_{1}+\mathsf{Q}_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2})]=0\\
c^{2}\mathfrak{p}_{2}^{\prime\prime}+\Lambda_{+}[\mathfrak{p}_{2}+\mathsf{Q}_{2}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2})]=0.\end{cases}

For
\displaystyle f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
, put

(\mathcal{I}_{+}f)(x):=\int_{0}^{1}f(x+s)\ ds\qquad\text{ and }\qquad(\mathcal{I}_{-}f)(x):=\int_{-1}^{0}f(x+s);

we met
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{-}
in (
3.5.2
).
Then

S^{1}-2+S^{-1}=\partial_{x}^{2}\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{I}_{-}.

Consequently, we may integrate the first equation in (
E.1.3
) twice to find that

c^{2}\mathfrak{p}_{1}-\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{I}_{-}\Lambda_{-}[\mathfrak{p}_{1}+\mathsf{Q}_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2})]

is constant for any solution
\displaystyle(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2})
of (
E.1.3
).
Thus this quantity is a first integral for the relative displacement traveling wave problem.
We took pains above to diagonalize using shift operators, not Fourier multipliers, so that this first integral is valid for as broad a class of profiles as possible, like the first integral in Iooss–Kirchgässner coordinates from Section
3.5
.

Suppose we choose the value of this first integral to be zero, very much along the lines of the remarks preceding Lemma
2.8
.
It turns out that the operator
\displaystyle c^{2}-\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{I}_{-}\Lambda_{-}
is invertible on the range of
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{I}_{-}
.
Put

\mathsf{M}_{-}(c):=(c^{2}-\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{I}_{-}\Lambda_{-})^{-1}\mathcal{I}_{+}\mathcal{I}_{-}\Lambda_{-}

to convert the first equation in (
E.1.3
) to

(E.1.4)

\mathfrak{p}_{1}+\mathsf{M}_{-}(c)\mathsf{Q}_{1}(\mathfrak{p}_{1},\mathfrak{p}_{2})=0.

This sort of “cancelation” is precisely the method of Friesecke and Pego
[
23
, Sec. 2, Eq. (2.3)]
for rearranging the monatomic traveling wave problem into a friendly form.
In the dimer, the more complicated structure of the equations occludes the first integral until after diagonalization.

E.2.
Long wave coordinates

Now introduce the long wave ansatz

(E.2.1)

\mathfrak{p}_{1}(x)=\nu^{2}\Rho_{1}(\nu{x}),\qquad\mathfrak{p}_{2}(x)=\nu^{2}\Rho_{2}(\nu{x}),\qquad\text{ and }\qquad c^{2}=c_{*}^{2}+\nu^{2},

where
\displaystyle c_{*}
is the dimer’s speed of sound, defined precisely in (
3.3.24
).
Following our notation in Section
1.5.3
, we use
\displaystyle\nu
for the long wave parameter here, not
\displaystyle\epsilon
.
After rewriting, relabeling, and condensing the terms and operators in (
E.1.3
) and (
E.1.4
), we arrive at

(E.2.2)

\begin{cases}\Rho_{1}+\mathsf{Q}_{1}^{\nu}(\Rho_{1},\Rho_{2})=0\\
\nu^{2}(c_{*}^{2}+\nu^{2})\Rho_{2}^{\prime\prime}+\mathsf{M}_{\nu}\Rho_{2}+\mathsf{Q}_{2}^{\nu}(\Rho_{1},\Rho_{2})=0.\end{cases}

The
\displaystyle\nu
-dependencies are all delicate, and we cheerfully omit the details here.

It turns out that (
E.2.2
) has the solution

(E.2.3)

\Rho_{1}(X)=\mathsf{C}_{1}\sech^{2}(\mathsf{C}_{2}X)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Rho_{2}(X)=0

at
\displaystyle\nu=0
.
The constants
\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{2}
depend on
\displaystyle\kappa
,
\displaystyle\beta
, and
\displaystyle w
; moreover, we need
\displaystyle\beta+\kappa^{3}\neq 0
.
Due to that
\displaystyle\nu
-dependent linear change of variables,
\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathsf{C}_{2}
are not quite the constants that appear in the
\displaystyle\sech^{2}
-type leading order solutions discussed in Section
1.5.3
.

E.3.
The naive perturbation problem

The natural instinct is to perturb from the
\displaystyle\nu=0
solution in (
E.2.3
) by setting

(E.3.1)

\Rho_{1}(X)=\mathsf{C}_{1}\sech^{2}(\mathsf{C}_{2}X)+\zeta_{1}(X)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Rho_{2}(X)=\zeta_{2}(X),

where
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
and
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
are “small” and exponentially localized.
Evaluating (
E.2.2
) at this ansatz leads to a system of two equations for
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
and
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
.

It is fairly easy to convert the first of these equations into a fixed point equation for
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
as a nonlinear function of itself,
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
, and
\displaystyle\nu
, but the second equation in (
E.2.2
) of course requires
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
to satisfy

(E.3.2)

\nu^{2}(c_{*}^{2}+\nu^{2})\zeta_{2}^{\prime\prime}+\mathsf{M}_{\nu}\zeta_{2}=-\mathsf{Q}_{2}^{\nu}(\mathsf{C}_{1}\sech^{2}(\mathsf{C}_{2}\cdot)+\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}).

A careful analysis of the roots of the symbol of the Fourier multiplier
\displaystyle\nu^{2}(c_{*}^{2}+\nu^{2})\partial_{X}^{2}+\mathsf{M}_{\nu}
shows that if

(E.3.3)

\varOmega_{\nu}:=\frac{\omega_{\sqrt{c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}+\nu^{2}}}}{\nu},

where
\displaystyle\omega_{\sqrt{c_{*}(\kappa,w)^{2}+\nu^{2}}}
was defined in Proposition
3.2
, then whenever
\displaystyle f
and
\displaystyle g
are any exponentially localized functions meeting

(E.3.4)

\nu^{2}(c_{*}^{2}+\nu^{2})f^{\prime\prime}+\mathsf{M}_{\nu}f=g,

the Fourier transform
\displaystyle\widehat{g}=\mathfrak{F}[g]
of
\displaystyle g
must also satisfy

(E.3.5)

\widehat{g}(\pm\varOmega_{\nu})=0.

This critical frequency
\displaystyle\varOmega_{\nu}=\mathcal{O}(\nu^{-1})
is closely tied to the “acoustic” and “optical” band structure of the dispersion relation for (
1.2.4
); we do not enter into a discussion of these terms here but refer the reader to
[
21
, Rem. 2.2]
and
[
6
, Ch. IV, Sec. 13, 15]
.

Thus it appears that the
two
unknown perturbation terms
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
and
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
must really meet
four
equations: the fixed point equation for
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
, the equation (
E.3.2
) for
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
, and the Fourier transform conditions

(E.3.6)

\mathfrak{F}[\mathsf{Q}_{2}^{\nu}(\mathsf{C}_{1}\sech^{2}(\mathsf{C}_{2}\cdot)+\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})](\pm\varOmega_{\nu})=0.

Consequently, the long wave problem now seems quite overdetermined.
However, by first restricting our analysis to mass or spring dimers, not the general dimer, we can reduce the number of equations by one.
Symmetry properties, which really are inherited from the precise choice of the eigenvectors in
\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(k)
back in (
E.1.2
), allow us to assume that
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
is even and
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
is odd in the case of the mass dimer and that both perturbation terms are even for the spring dimer.
This allows us to replace the two equations in (
E.3.6
) by just the Fourier transform at
\displaystyle+\varOmega_{\nu}
.

E.4.
Beale’s nanopteron ansatz

We emphasize that the following work holds equally well for the mass dimer or the spring dimer, but not (to date) the general dimer.

Despite the reduction from symmetry, we still have three equations and two unknowns, and here we turn to Beale’s insights.
One does not need to view the system (
E.2.2
) strictly within a universe of exponentially localized functions.
Rather, the same symbol properties that gave the solvability condition (
E.3.5
) for (
E.3.4
) mean that the exact sinusoidal functions

\Rho_{1}(X)=0\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Rho_{2}(X)=\psi(\varOmega_{\nu}X)

solve the linearization (at
\displaystyle\Rho_{1}=\Rho_{2}=0
) of (
E.2.2
).
Here
\displaystyle\psi(s)=\sin(s)
for the mass dimer and
\displaystyle\psi(s)=\sin(s)
for the spring dimer.
It is possible to use a quantitative version of the classical Crandall–Rabinowitz–Zeidler “bifurcation from a simple eigenvalue” technique
[
12
,
86
]
to extend these solutions into a family of periodic solutions to the full nonlinear problem (
E.2.2
) of the form

(E.4.1)

\Rho_{1}(X)=a\phi_{1,\nu}^{a}(X)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Rho_{2}(X)=a\phi_{2,\nu}^{a}(X)=a\psi(\varOmega_{\nu}^{a}X)+\mathcal{O}(a^{2})

for
\displaystyle a\in\mathbb{R}
sufficiently small.
The profiles
\displaystyle\phi_{k,\nu}^{a}
are
\displaystyle 2\pi
-periodic with frequency
\displaystyle\varOmega_{\nu}^{a}=\varOmega_{\nu}+\mathcal{O}(a^{2})
; it then follows from (
E.3.3
) and (
D.1.7
) that
\displaystyle\varOmega_{\nu}^{a}
agrees to leading order with the frequency of the spatial dynamics-derived periodic profiles.
In a subtle way, symmetry nicely reduces the number of equations that must be solved in the Crandall–Rabinowitz–Zeidler-type bifurcation that constructs the periodic solutions, very much along the lines of what occurs in Lombardi’s periodic construction (Appendix
D.4
).

Beale’s success in overcoming the analogous difficulties in the water wave problem
[
3
]
hinged on incorporating the periodic solutions (
E.4.1
) into a revision of the original exponentially localized ansatz (
E.3.1
) by positing instead

\Rho_{1}(X)=\mathsf{C}_{1}\sech^{2}(\mathsf{C}_{2}X)+\zeta_{1}(X)+a\phi_{1,\nu}^{a}(X)\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\Rho_{2}(X)=\zeta_{2}(X)+a\phi_{2,\nu}^{a}(X).

Now there are three unknowns: the two perturbation terms
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
and
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
and the “amplitude” coefficient
\displaystyle a
.
Careful manipulations of the new versions of (
E.3.2
) and (
E.3.6
) that result from this ansatz lead to fixed point equations for
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}
and
\displaystyle a
, whereas previously we only had a good equation for
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}
.
A quantitative contraction mapping argument, taking into account an unusually bad Lipschitz constant, produces, for
\displaystyle\nu
sufficiently small, the solutions that ultimately lead to (
1.3.3
).

E.5.
The periodic amplitude coefficient

In particular, if
\displaystyle\zeta_{1}^{\nu}
,
\displaystyle\zeta_{2}^{\nu}
, and
\displaystyle a_{\nu}
are the solutions, then
\displaystyle a_{\nu}
satisfies

(E.5.1)

a_{\nu}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\mathcal{I}_{\nu}[\eta_{1}^{\nu},\eta_{2}^{\nu},a_{\nu}](X)\sin(\varOmega_{\nu}X)\ dX.

The function
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\nu}[\eta_{1},\eta_{2},a]
is as smooth as the dimer’s potentials
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}
and the input functions
\displaystyle\eta_{1}
and
\displaystyle\eta_{2}
are; a bootstrapping argument shows that the solutions
\displaystyle\eta_{1}^{\nu}
and
\displaystyle\eta_{2}^{\nu}
are as smooth as
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}
.
When
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{1}
and
\displaystyle\mathcal{V}_{2}
are
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, so is
\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\nu}[\eta_{1}^{\nu},\eta_{2}^{\nu},a_{\nu}]
, and so a Riemann–Lebesgue estimate on the oscillatory integral (
E.5.1
) shows that
\displaystyle a_{\nu}
is small beyond all algebraic orders of
\displaystyle\nu
, which is the estimate (
1.3.5
) above.

Remark E.1
.

More precisely, this Riemann–Lebesgue estimate states that if
\displaystyle f
and its first
\displaystyle r
derivatives vanish exponentially fast and if
\displaystyle\varOmega_{\nu}=\mathcal{O}(\nu^{-1})
, then

\left|\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}f(X)e^{-i\varOmega_{\nu}X}\ dX\right|\leq C_{r}\nu^{r}.

See Lemma A.5 in
[
21
]
.
The fixed point construction of
\displaystyle\eta_{1}^{\nu}
and
\displaystyle\eta_{2}^{\nu}
above does not require the spring potentials to be
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\infty}
, merely
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{r}
for a sufficiently large
\displaystyle r
.
And so if the potentials are
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{r}
but not
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{r+1}
, then
\displaystyle\eta_{1}^{\nu}
,
\displaystyle\eta_{2}^{\nu}
are only
\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{r}
, and thus
\displaystyle a_{\nu}
is not necessarily small beyond all orders.

E.6.
Further dialogue with the Lombardi–spatial dynamics method

The principal contrast between Lombardi’s nanopteron construction and Beale’s is that Lombardi fixes the amplitude of his periodic ripple and then runs a fixed point argument for the exponentially localized error alone, whereas Beale runs a fixed point argument for both the error and the amplitude.
Secondarily, Lombardi is required to accept a small phase shift in the periodic terms, whereas the phase shift is optional for Beale and, to all appearances, not necessarily small.
Based on the Amick–Toland method
[
2
]
and the adapted outline by Faver and Wright
[
21
, Sec. 7]
, it should be possible to select first an arbitrary phase shift and then run Beale’s method.
See
[
62
, Rem. 7.3.31]
for a comparison along these lines in Lombardi’s own words.

Another contrast is that the original relative displacement problem (
1.1.6
) is not translation invariant in the sense that the position problem (
1.1.4
) is.
That is,
\displaystyle\{r_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
solves (
1.1.6
), it need not be the case that
\displaystyle\{\widetilde{r}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
does, too, where
\displaystyle\widetilde{r}_{j}(t):=r_{j}(t)+d_{1}t+d_{2}
,
\displaystyle d_{1}
,
\displaystyle d_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
This contrasts with the position invariances described in Remark
3.6
.

Diagonalization has not been used in the other applications of Beale’s method to FPUT and MiM lattices
[
36
,
19
,
17
]
.
Instead, those traveling wave problems possess a “mean-zero” symmetry in one of their components; roughly, the Fourier mode at zero of one component of the problem is always zero.
This does occur in the first equation in (
E.1.3
), but we had to exploit it further with the “Friesecke–Pego cancelation.”
In those other “material limits,” the mean-zero symmetry has a host of technical applications; in particular, it reduces by one the dimension of the kernel of the linearization of the periodic problem, thereby facilitating, in part, the modified bifurcation-from-a-simple-eigenvalue approach.

Finally, we note two possible disadvantages of the spatial dynamics/Lombardi method relative to Beale’s.
First, the nature of Lombardi’s rescaling (Lemma
D.2
) requires, in the spring dimer case,
\displaystyle\beta+\kappa^{3}\neq 0
.
However, this is necessary to apply Lombardi’s periodic construction.
In
[
16
]
, it was possible to produce the periodics even with
\displaystyle\beta+\kappa^{3}=0
.

Second, as we noted in Section
1.3
, most other applications of Beale’s ansatz
[
36
,
17
,
19
]
have produced nanopterons whose localized core is
\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(1)
in the problem’s small parameter.
However, Lombardi’s long wave scaling ensures that his cores are indeed small in the relevant small parameter; this is apparent in (
D.1.4
).
Although the amplitude estimates of Beale’s method have been, heretofore, less precise than those of Lombardi, it seems that Beale’s method affords more flexibility as to the precise structure of the nanopteron.
